real time. Arguably more honest than today's version regardless of
this refactor's fate — reflects "what Claude is doing" rather than
"what's been recorded," and can't desync the way marker-based state
can, since it's just reading append-only files.

Explicit instruction, added 2026-08-06: don't stop at a bare
event-name swap. Now that a pending-queue-inspection tool
([[id:63a642c7-b04d-42dc-b6ec-3fb13df3ae04][63a642c7]]) exists in this
same plan — and, since
[[id:c9292ce8-8fb5-41c0-8de1-bbf74ffa7fce][c9292ce8]] was cancelled,
is the *only* place queue state gets surfaced outside the review
command — consider whether the statusline should surface that
same information glanceably from the terminal — e.g. a pending-review
count ("3 updates awaiting review"), or a visible distinction between
"applied" and "queued, not yet reviewed" for whatever heading it
names — rather than just relocating today's single clocked-in/out
label to a different data source with the same one-line shape.

*Third requirement, folded in from
[[id:4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5][4cda6bf7]] on 2026-08-14,
which was cancelled in favour of this heading:* the label must
distinguish the *states*, not just the clock. Today's binary collapses
four distinct situations into two words — =PLANNING= and =DOING= both
read "clocked in" though one is still designing and the other
implementing; =WAIT= and =DONE= both read "clocked out" though one is
blocked and the other finished.

That heading proposed getting the keyword from =org-get-todo-state= at
the clock marker. *Do not do that here* — it is the reason it was
cancelled rather than merged as-is. The on-disk keyword lags the queue
by an entire review pass, so it would report a heading as =TODO= while
this session has already queued =DOING= on it. The keyword this
statusline should show is *the one the queue last recorded*, which is
the same source everything else in this heading reads from, and which is
also the only version that answers "what is Claude attending to" rather
than "what did the last review pass write down."

Practical consequence for whoever builds this: the five existing
=--statusline-task-string= regression tests assert on the literal
"clocked in"/"clocked out" strings and will need rewriting. Doing both
changes here means rewriting them once rather than twice.

[[id:d1cf852a-696a-4195-a4a7-039439c1f4b7][d1cf852a]] out on 2026-08-13.
Decoupling live visibility is worth doing whether or not the epic ever
closes, and the epic closes whether or not this is done.

** TODO Read power assertions as a review-time attribution signal :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 15:52]
:END:

CLAUDE.md records =pmset -g log= as "not yet attempted" and frames the
per-app power assertions as noise obscuring clean sleep/wake
transitions. For *this* question the framing is backwards: a laptop that
never sleeps emits zero transitions and a rich assertion record, and the
assertion holder is the answer.

Tried 2026-08-11 against a known-answer window. The log named the
competing activity exactly:

: 14:01:56  Created   NoDisplaySleepAssertion  (zoom.us, PID 58601)
: 14:42:14  Released  NoDisplaySleepAssertion  00:40:17

The user's own estimate was "roughly 14:00 to roughly 14:45" — the log
was right to the second, and the 40m17s sits entirely inside the 54m11s
permission-block of
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]].

*Not a validated method, on one observation.* Zoom happened to hold a
cleanly-named assertion; =coreaudiod= held 158 of the 361 entries in the
same window and is a far noisier proxy. An assertion also is not
inattention — people work through calls. The most it can honestly do is
prompt the human reviewer, never decide.

*Retention bounds it:* the log went back ~7 days (earliest 2026-08-04).
That puts a deadline on "the human applies at a moment of their own
choosing."

Revises the disposition recorded in
[[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]]'s neighbourhood,
which proposed closing the =pmset= lead as won't-do — correct for the
stale-interval guess heuristic, wrong for attribution.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* as a capability rather than a requirement. Note it is
explicitly *not* the =pmset= lead that
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] closed as
won't-do: that one guessed when a stale clock stopped, this one assigns
an existing span to a heading at review time. Same data source,
different question, and only the first was refuted.

** TODO Background-task notifications write phantom resume guideposts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       09c134c4-31eb-4f6c-aa10-f836d1d28f16
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 17:46]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 17:47] \\
  phantom resume inflates human-present time
:END:

Observed 2026-08-12 during the subagent experiment behind
[[id:0d789b68-faca-4911-868a-fdd70acbfd40][0d789b68]]. A background
task completing re-invokes the model, which fires =UserPromptSubmit=,
which appends a =resume= guidepost — with no user having sent anything:

: 17:22:41  resume  UserPromptSubmit   (agent 1 finished ~17:21:47)
: 17:23:50  resume  UserPromptSubmit   (agent 2 finished ~17:23:15)

The events are byte-identical to a genuine prompt's; no field
distinguishes them. This matters because CLAUDE.md defines the
pause/resume stream as tracking *active work time only* — "paused the
moment Claude Code stops and is waiting on the user, resumed the moment
the user sends the next prompt." A robot wake-up now reads as the human
returning to the keyboard, and since
=--aggregate-guideposts= reconstructs human spans from exactly these
timestamps, the effect is to inflate apparent human-present time in
every =:suggested:= interval that spans a background completion.

Affects any background task, not just subagents. Note the guidepost
carries =agent_id: nil= even when the notification came from an agent —
the hook fires in the parent's turn, not the agent's — so filtering on
=agent_id= is *not* the fix.

Not yet investigated: whether the hook payload distinguishes a real
submission from a task-completion re-invocation at all. If it does not,
the honest options are to accept the inflation and document it, or to
stop treating =resume= as evidence of human presence.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* alongside
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]], which it pairs
with directly — both are about guideposts that are missing or phantom,
and [[id:d1cf852a-696a-4195-a4a7-039439c1f4b7][d1cf852a]] already notes
that a bracket bounds the damage from either. Filed under the epic by
proximity rather than membership: the queue is complete without it.

** MAYBE Add a third retention tier for completed work as a chronological changelog :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4c0044bf-1b6c-44ea-807a-8f9e3523e3fd
:CREATED:  [2026-08-07 Fri 09:41]
:END:

Captured 2026-08-07 from conversation. Proposal: TODO.org (live) →
DONE.org (archive) → a terse, permanent, conventional-timeline changelog,
so DONE.org can be pruned or semantically compressed as work recedes
without losing the record entirely.

*Measured first, because the diagnosis moves:* DONE.org holds 22 entries
and 955 lines, every one archived on =2026-07-29= or =-07-30= — archiving
has not run since. Meanwhile TODO.org is 2444 lines and still carries
*13* =DONE=/=CANCELLED= headings, the oldest at line 7. The pressure is
not tier 2 overflowing; it is tier 1→2 having stalled, so completed work
accumulates in the live file. A third tier added now would relieve none
of that. Restore the archiving cadence first and re-measure — tier 3 may
not be needed for a long time.

*If built anyway, three constraints:*
- *Referential integrity is the real risk.* 21 distinct =[[id:...]]=
  links point into archived headings today, from other org files,
  CLAUDE.md, and the auto-memory folder — two of them added
  2026-08-07 citing =6a6d5b4e=. A conventional Markdown changelog has no
  stable per-entry identifier, so pruning breaks every inbound link
  silently. Any tier-3 format must carry the original =:ID:=, or leave an
  ID-bearing stub behind in DONE.org.
- *Git is already a chronological changelog*, so the question is what
  this adds. The honest answer: git records what *changed*; DONE.org
  records what was *learned* and why the obvious approach failed. That is
  the part with no other home — and it means tier 3's content should be
  biased toward decisions and dead ends, not shipped features, which git
  already covers.
- *Compression is where this project's signature failure mode lives.*
  The most valuable sentence in =6a6d5b4e= is that the initial hypothesis
  was a red herring — precisely what terse summarization drops first,
  because it does not look like an outcome. Editorial rule: keep what
  would change a future decision, drop what is re-derivable from the
  code. Model-driven compression without human review is the same
  confidently-wrong pattern [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
  exists to eliminate; make it diffable and reviewed.

*Format: stay in org, don't leave for Markdown.* =org-archive.el= already
supports =datetree/= in =org-archive-location= (org-archive.el:284), so
=#+ARCHIVE: DONE.org::datetree/* Done= buys chronological structure with
no new code, while preserving =[[id:]]= resolution, =org-ql=
queryability, and tags. A =CHANGELOG.md= discards all of that for the
keepachangelog convention, which is release-oriented — and this project
has no releases. Checked per
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s standing
instruction to exhaust org's own machinery first.

Open: what triggers a prune (size, age, or deliberate act — probably
deliberate); whether tier 3 is its own =CHANGELOG.org= or a datetree
inside DONE.org; and whether anything should enumerate inbound =[[id:]]=
references before a prune, since nothing does today.

** TODO Re-measure the guidepost gap threshold against a month of queue data :code:research:
SCHEDULED: <2026-09-13 Sun +1m>
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 12:20]
:END:

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
on 2026-08-13.* It was filed there because the threshold it re-measures
belongs to that epic's queue — but it carries a =+1m= repeater and so
never reaches =DONE=, which meant the epic could not either, and
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] is blocked on that
epic. A recurring measurement has no terminal state and therefore no
completable parent.
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
  scheduled re-measure, one month out
:END:

Scheduled rather than left to memory: the finding below is real but
thin, and the failure mode is adopting a four-day number as permanent
because nobody re-ran it.

*The survey, 2026-08-13.* 422 queue events, 2026-08-10 to 08-13, across
six sessions. Measured the *queue* rather than =:LOGBOOK:= deliberately:
=:LOGBOOK:= intervals are reconstructions produced by the very threshold
under test, so measuring them would partly measure the parameter.

Decomposed each session's stream into the two gaps that mean different
things:

- =resume= -> =pause= — how long the agent worked. n=111, median *81s*,
  p90 *211s*, one outlier over an hour (the concurrent subagent run).
- =pause= -> =resume= — how long the human took to reply. n=150, median
  *181s*.

*The result, and it is sharper than expected: the second is bimodal with
an empty band between the modes.* Sorted gaps run =… 949, 971, 1061=
and then jump straight to =2070, 2103, 2135 …=. **No gap anywhere in the
corpus falls between 1061s and 2070s.** A threshold sweep sees the same
hole from the other side: span count falls steeply, then sits flat at 29
across 1200-1800s, then resumes falling. Every value inside the band
produces an identical reconstruction.

Acted on immediately: =claude-code-ide-org-guidepost-gap-threshold= 900
-> *1200s*, the round number inside the band, at the start of the
plateau with margin either side. 900 sat just below the band and split
five spans nothing justifies splitting.

*A stated assumption the data contradicted, which is the more useful
half.* The survey was proposed on the premise that silence from ~18:00
to ~08:00 is almost certainly AFK. True, but not where the long gaps
are: of 19 gaps over 2000s, *11 start inside working hours* — 07:00,
10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 15:00, 16:00, 17:00 — including the two longest
(151 and 158 minutes, starting 12:59 and 15:34). Daytime absence is
indistinguishable from nighttime absence. Gap *length* separates the
modes cleanly; clock hour does not. Recorded against
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], whose
working-hours guess rests on exactly the premise this refutes.

*What to re-run on the scheduled date*, and the reason the number is not
yet settled: n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually
intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact
of that. Re-derive the histogram and the sweep; if the band has moved or
closed, retune again and say so here rather than defending 1200.

*This is a moving target, not a one-off calibration* — the user's own
framing, 2026-08-13, and it changes what the re-measure is for.
Proficiency, setup and work objectives all evolve, and the habits
generating these gaps evolve with them. A faster operator, a different
mix of long-running subagent work, or a stretch of shallow triage rather
than deep implementation would each shift the modes and could close the
band entirely. So the finding above describes *August 2026*, not this
project. The scheduled task is not "confirm 1200 was right"; it is
"re-derive, and expect a different answer."

*Made a =+1m= repeater at the user's instruction, 2026-08-13*, which is
the honest encoding of "moving target": a one-shot date would have
implied the number gets settled once. It does not. Each occurrence
re-derives the histogram and the sweep against whatever the preceding
month's habits actually were, and either confirms the current value or
moves it.

Practical note for whoever handles an occurrence: marking it =DONE=
rolls the =SCHEDULED= stamp forward a month and returns the heading to
=TODO= — org's own repeater behavior — so the record of *that* month's
finding has to be written into this body before closing it, or it is
gone. Append; do not overwrite the previous month's numbers.

Charts from the original survey are an artifact, not committed here —
regenerate rather than trusting a stale rendering.

** TODO Institute a catch-all heading for cross-cutting meta-work :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a1d63f52-4c07-4b9e-8f31-6d2b90c4e7a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 12:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 14:10]--[2026-08-14 Fri 14:28] =>  0:18
- [2026-08-14 Fri 14:10]--[2026-08-14 Fri 14:28] reallocated from e51d6ba1's mis-suggested span
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-10: a standing pseudo-task to clock
against for grooming, capturing, "what's next" review, and similar
activity that is real work but belongs to no single tracked task.
Captured as TODO on a recommendation, not as a settled decision.

*The concrete argument for it, from this session's own data.* Of the
first ten events in this session's queue, *eight* were =pause=/=resume=
guideposts that reduced to nothing at review time. Guideposts carry no
heading; they inherit whichever one the surrounding
=clock_in=/=clock_out= last named, so guideposts recorded while no clock
is open are unattributable and silently dropped. Meta-work is precisely
when no clock is open. So today that time is not merely misattributed --
it is invisible, which is in direct tension with this project's second
stated goal of trustworthy tracking of where attention actually went.

A standing task, clocked during that work, gives those guideposts a
bracket to attach to. Nothing else has to change for it to work.

*Check org's own mechanism first, per
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]].* Org already has
a name for this concept: =org-clock-default-task=, set with
=org-clock-mark-default-task=, a designated fallback task you can clock
into directly. This should almost certainly use that rather than invent
a parallel convention -- exactly the reflex c084553c exists to enforce.

*Two real risks, neither disqualifying:*
- *The dumping-ground failure.* If ambiguous time drifts here by
  default, the record gets *less* informative, not more -- a large
  bucket labelled "misc" answers no question worth asking. Scope it to
  genuinely cross-cutting work, and treat the review pass as the place
  to re-attribute a span that turned out to be about one task.
- *It never reaches DONE.* A permanent open task sits in a sibling group
  alongside the single-NEXT-per-level triggers
  (=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=,
  =--trigger-demote-conflicting-next=). Check how those behave with a
  sibling that is deliberately never finished before relying on it. It
  also never archives, so its =:LOGBOOK:= grows without bound -- probably
  fine for a pure time record, but it is a decision, not an oversight.

Independent of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] and
useful either way: attribution is resolved from queued
=clock_in=/=clock_out= events both before and after the cutover, so the
bracket does the same job in both worlds.


*Refined 2026-08-10, and it narrows what this heading is for.* The user
proposed the general principle: grooming, defining, amending and
adjusting a future task is *activity on that task*, and should be clocked
against it — leaving its TODO state untouched. Recorded here because it
answers most of what a catch-all was being considered for.

*This was never stated anywhere, though nothing forbade it.* CLAUDE.md's
transition rules say a transition *to* =DOING= opens a clock and one
*from* =DOING= closes it. They never say the converse — that a running
clock implies =DOING= — so clocking a =TODO= heading has always been
legal by the letter and simply undescribed. Confirmed safe in practice
the same day: =org-clock-in-switch-to-state= is nil here, so clocking in
does not touch the keyword, and this heading's own editorial session was
clocked against =02aaae22= while it stayed literally =TODO=.

*Why it is better than a bucket, where it applies.* Attribution is exact:
time spent working out what a task should be lands on that task, next to
the reasoning it produced, and shows up in any report scoped to it.
A catch-all would answer "how much meta-work happened" but never "which
task was I circling."

*So the two are complementary, and this heading shrinks to the residue:*
work that genuinely belongs to no single task — "what's next?" surveys
across the whole backlog, cross-cutting review, triage before a task
exists to attach to. That residue is real but much smaller than the
original framing assumed, which also blunts the dumping-ground risk noted
above: with per-task grooming as the default, anything reaching the
catch-all is there because it truly has no home.

*Consequence worth accepting deliberately:* a =TODO= heading can now
accrue real clock time without ever having been =DOING=. That is correct
— deciding what a task is *is* work — but it means "time spent" and
"was worked on" stop being the same question, and any future reporting
needs to keep them apart.

*Needs a rule in CLAUDE.md* under "State transition rules", stating that
a clock may run on a heading in any state, and that grooming attaches to
the task being groomed. Distinct from
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]], which is scoped to
documenting the post-cutover architecture; this is a general convention
that holds regardless of the refactor's fate.


*Alternative captured 2026-08-11, and it narrows this heading further:*
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] proposes that the review buffer
surface unattributed guideposts and let the human assign them to a
heading retrospectively, rather than requiring anyone to clock into a
standing task in advance. If that lands, this heading is no longer a
mechanism to remember — it is just a convenient assignment target for
spans that genuinely belong to no task.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on 2026-08-13.*

It arose from that epic's work but is not required for it to complete: the queue
can be finished and correct without ever gaining capture/amend events. It also
has its own plan file, its own scope decisions and its own deferred phases — a
child with a plan file of its own is usually an epic wearing a child's clothes.
Left as a level-2 task rather than declared an epic, since it has no children
yet; it will become one naturally if capture and amend split.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/capture-amend-queue.md][Plan]]

*Plan approved 2026-08-13, scoped by two decisions the user made during
planning:* v1 covers *capture and amend* only — the structural writers
(=org_refile=, =org_archive=, =org_move_sibling=, =org_sort_children=)
stay immediate — and capture *writes through when the file is free*,
queueing only when it is busy, so today's immediacy survives and only
contended writes defer. That is the user's original lock idea made
non-blocking, and it supersedes the "always queue" reading of step 3
below.

Note the plan lives in its own file rather than this conversation's
=hazy-moseying-sky.md=: that path is already the permanent link for [[id:6b1e73c4-25da-4f0e-8a51-9c0d3f7ab214][6b1e73c4]] and
[[id:f290161c-f9e7-4bfb-9adf-4c5630d4c5ae][f290161c]], and Claude Code reuses one plan file per conversation, so planning
here overwrote it. Restored from =plans/= and the new plan given its own name,
so both older links still resolve to the plan they were written for.

*Read by the user 2026-08-13* — both the original draft below and the
=create-lockfiles= research appended to step 1. The "study this before
signing off" reminder it was captured under is discharged.

*No verdict was recorded, and none should be inferred.* The user said
only that they had reviewed it and that the heading could be amended
accordingly; they did not approve the recommendation, reject it, or
choose between its three steps. So this remains a proposal Claude
drafted, not an agreed design, and the three load-bearing claims are
still open to challenge: that a *blocking* lock is the wrong primitive,
that the underlying need is a missing queue rather than a missing lock,
and that the ordering below is the right one. Anyone acting on this
should get an explicit decision first.

*The ask, in the user's words:* so many times they want to ask a
question or make a proposal while something else is in flight, which
often leads to something new to capture or amend. The literal proposal
was a file or Emacs buffer lock/semaphore on =TODO.org= and friends,
causing concurrent sessions to wait their turn while another holds it.

*What actually collides today.*

The queue already removed most of the contention: =todo= and clock
events are append-only, per-session, written blind with =O_APPEND=, so
two sessions cannot corrupt each other there by construction. What
remains is *direct writes to the file*:

- =org_capture=, =org_refile=, =org_archive=, =org_move_sibling= and
  =org_sort_children=, which all still =save-buffer= immediately
- the org skill's prose edits via Claude Code's =Edit= tool, which
  bypass Emacs entirely and write to disk
- the user's own hand-edits in the live buffer
- =.git/index=, a separate incident already owned by
  [[id:2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c][2aeb65d6]]

Not hypothetical: on 2026-08-13 two edits in one session hit "the file
had been modified on disk since you last read it". The ad-hoc
mitigation in use — checking =buffer-modified-p= before touching the
file — is a hand-rolled version of what this heading proposes.

*Why a blocking lock looks like the wrong primitive.*

Recorded as the argument against, because "why not just take a lock" is
the obvious question a future reader will ask.

1. *Waiting reintroduces the failure this architecture exists to avoid.*
   A session blocked on a lock burns wall-clock with no way to report
   why, and waits forever if the holder crashed. Same shape as the
   non-interactive =emacsclient= hang that pushed this project to
   append-only.
2. *The contention is asymmetric.* The human is the authoritative
   writer; Claude sessions should yield to them and never the reverse. A
   symmetric semaphore models the wrong relationship.
3. *It cannot be enforced where it matters most.* The =Edit= tool
   consults neither Emacs nor any lockfile, so any lock is advisory for
   exactly the writer most likely to be mid-flight. A mechanism that
   looks like mutual exclusion but is not is worse than an honest
   advisory one.
4. *Stale locks are stale clocks again.* An owner, a TTL and a recovery
   path — the subsystem
   [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is currently
   deciding whether to retire, rebuilt for a new resource.

*The reframe this rests on — the part most worth challenging.*

The ask may not be mutual exclusion at all. Note where the user's own
sentence ends: the interruption *leads to something new to capture or
amend*. That is a missing queue, not a missing lock — state changes
already work that way; capture and amend do not.

If that reframe is wrong — if the real need is genuinely "stop the
other writer" rather than "do not lose my thought" — then the
recommendation below is wrong too, and a fail-fast lock alone is the
answer.

*Proposed, cheapest first.*

1. *Turn on =create-lockfiles=.* Measured 2026-08-13: it is =nil=, Doom's
   default, so Emacs's own mechanism is currently off entirely. One
   line. It will not stop the =Edit= tool, but it makes Emacs-mediated
   collisions visible rather than silent. Checked before proposing
   anything bespoke, per
   [[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s standing
   instruction.

   *Why Doom disables it, checked 2026-08-13 at the user's request.*
   =lisp/doom-emacs.el:462= sets it under a =Backup & autosave files=
   heading, sharing one comment with =make-backup-files=: "Don't
   generate backups or lockfiles. ... This is better suited to version
   control, and I don't want world-readable copies of potentially
   sensitive material floating around our filesystem." Older Doom
   (=core/core-editor.el=) worded it the same way.

   That rationale is about *backups*, and does not actually describe a
   lock file. Per the Emacs Lisp manual, a lock file is a symlink named
   =.#FILE= whose *target* is the string =user@host.pid:boot=; it holds
   no contents of the edited file at all. So the "copies of sensitive
   material" argument does not apply to the thing it is being used to
   justify — lockfiles were bundled in with backups under one heading
   and inherited a comment written for backups. A residual privacy point
   survives (the target leaks username, host and PID), but that is a
   much smaller claim than the one made.

   The real-world objection in the wider community is *tooling*, not
   privacy: =.#FILE= is a dangling symlink, and file watchers and build
   tools choke on it (e.g. =ember-cli= issue #6527, "=(setq
   create-lockfiles nil)= shouldn't be necessary for Emacs usage"). That
   risk is low for this repo — nothing here watches the tree — but it is
   the reason to check before flipping it globally rather than
   per-directory.

   *And one point actively in favor, which is why this step is listed at
   all:* on detecting a lock, Emacs calls =ask-user-about-lock=, which
   offers grab / ignore / abort. It *asks*; it does not wait. That is
   precisely the fail-fast shape argued for in step 2, already
   implemented upstream.
2. *A fail-fast advisory lock in the MCP write tools* — a refusal, not a
   wait: "another session is writing TODO.org; queued instead." Fast
   failure is debuggable and cannot deadlock.
3. *Extend the queue to capture/amend proposals.* The interjection
   becomes an event, nothing waits, and the existing human review pass
   applies it — which also makes it reviewable rather than immediate,
   the property already chosen deliberately for state changes.

Steps 2 and 3 overlap: if 3 lands, 2 may be unnecessary, since there
would be nothing left that needs to write immediately.


** TODO Bracket queued spans with WAIT transitions, so the heading guess stops latching :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d1cf852a-696a-4195-a4a7-039439c1f4b7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 18:34]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 18:34] \\
  captured from conversation, awaiting your read
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 19:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 19:25] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-13 Thu 19:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 19:25] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 08:21]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] =>  0:43
- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:21]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

*Noticed 2026-08-13, in conversation, after the pattern had already been
used twice without anyone naming it.* =WAIT= has quietly acquired a
second, narrower meaning here: the resting state at the end of a Claude
work increment, meaning "the ball is in the human's court". That is not
what the =#+TODO:= table says it means (generic "Blocked or waiting on
someone"), and the difference is load-bearing.

*Why it matters for the queue.* The suggestion machinery's known defect
is that the guess *latches*: measured live 2026-08-13, three spans
covering eight hours all suggested a heading that had long gone quiet.
It latches because a =clock_in= sets a lane's current heading and
*nothing ever unsets it* — there is no event meaning "Claude stopped
working on this."

A =DOING -> WAIT= transition is exactly that event, and it has three
properties a heading guess does not:

1. *A hard right edge.* Guideposts after it provably do not belong to
   that heading. That alone kills the latch.
2. *The =WAIT -> DOING= gap is human time, not attention.* By
   construction that interval is "the human had not answered yet" — it
   is not work on the task, yet today it is clustered into a span and
   offered for assignment like any other.
3. *It is already in the queue.* =todo= events carry state and timestamp
   whether or not anyone has applied them, so a reader can bracket spans
   without the file having moved. No new event kind, no schema change.

Composes with [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]:
commits and =:CREATED:= stamps inside a span's window are the evidence,
and =WAIT= edges are the bounds on which windows are worth looking at.
It is also a cheaper, mechanical partial answer to
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]], which proposes
asking Claude to narrate the same gaps.

*Two things to settle before this becomes a rule.*

- *The keyword is ambiguous.* A =WAIT= meaning "waiting on a vendor to
  email back" carries none of this. The discriminator is probably not
  the keyword at all but the event's =source=/=session_id=: a =WAIT= a
  *session* queued is a Claude/human handoff, one a human set by hand is
  not. That distinction already exists in every queue line and is
  currently unused.
- *It is only as reliable as its consistency.* A session that ends
  leaving a heading =DOING= has a silently missing bracket, and reading
  absence as "still working" is the wrong default in exactly the case
  that matters. Worth deciding whether that is a lint
  ([[id:3bd3402b-3ab6-4ace-b20b-3ff84caa3fb2][3bd3402b]]) or a hook.

*Provenance, recorded because it bears on how much weight to put on
this:* the pattern came from the user's own correction, not from design.
[[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] was set to =WAIT=
at their explicit instruction on 2026-08-13, and the same call was then
applied to [[id:b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691][b5f94b88]] by
imitation. Two instances, one person, one afternoon — an observed habit
worth testing, not a measured regularity.

May also bound the damage from
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]] and
[[id:09c134c4-31eb-4f6c-aa10-f836d1d28f16][09c134c4]], both of which are
about guideposts that are missing or phantom: a bracket constrains what
a stray guidepost can be attributed to, even when the guidepost itself
is wrong.

*Correction, 2026-08-14: the claim above that "nothing ever unsets it" is
wrong, and was already wrong when this heading was written.*

=claude-code-ide-org--review-suggest-heading= (=config.el= line 3097 as
of today) already clears the active heading on any =todo= event carrying
=DONE=, =CANCELLED=, =WAIT=, =TODO=, =NEXT= or =MAYBE= — that is, on
every keyword except =DOING= and =PLANNING=. It arrived in =3c6b3f2=
("Implement 3d0487f4: unassigned spans, with a suggested heading") on
2026-08-13, hours *before* this heading was captured, and the
latching-guess comment sits directly above it. Found by grep on
2026-08-14 while inventorying keyword touch points for
[[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]; it was not
verified when this heading was written, which is the actual lesson.

So the mechanism this heading proposes building *exists*, and it is
already keyed the way the design conclusion below recommends — on any
transition out of =DOING=/=PLANNING=, not on =WAIT= specifically. Two
things survive the correction, and they are what is left of the idea:

1. *The gap is data, not logic.* The bracket only fires when a =todo=
   event exists to fire it. A session that ends leaving a heading =DOING=
   emits nothing, so the guess keeps latching — which is exactly the
   "only as reliable as its consistency" caveat recorded above, now
   promoted from a caveat to the whole remaining problem. Whether that is
   answered by a lint
   ([[id:3bd3402b-3ab6-4ace-b20b-3ff84caa3fb2][3bd3402b]]), a =Stop=-hook
   transition, or an inferred bracket at review time is undecided.
2. *The denylist should be an allowlist.* Testing for "not =DOING= and
   not =PLANNING=" instead of naming six keywords would make it immune to
   any future change to the keyword set — including the =REVIEW=
   proposal now recorded on
   [[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]], which would
   otherwise need remembering to add a seventh string here. A keyword
   forgotten in that list fails silently, by latching.

*What this heading is now about,* therefore: not "build a bracket" but
"make sure a bracket event always gets emitted, and make the test
robust to the keyword set changing." The =WAIT= framing in the title is
vestigial — the keyword was never the load-bearing part, and the title
should be retitled if this heading is picked up.

** TODO Type a span's annotation at review time, not by hand-editing it after :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8028e875-3032-4ade-949e-414d9861b928
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 12:27]
:END:

*Observed 2026-08-14, in the file rather than in the abstract.* The user
hand-edited an applied annotation on
[[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]] after the fact,
rewriting =suggested span, accepted at review= to =task definition=. The
commit record confirms the correction was right — =d34c809= landed at
08:58, before the 09:09--09:42 span opened, so that interval really was
task definition and not the implementation it sat next to.

*The mechanism, read rather than assumed*
(=claude-code-ide-org--review-annotation-label=, =config.el= line 3298 as
of today). It returns one of three things:

- the enclosing =clock_in='s note, when the span inherited one — which is
  where a genuinely informative line like =show commits and :CREATED: in
  span windows= comes from;
- the empty string, for a bracketed span whose =clock_in= carried no note;
- =assigned at review= / =suggested span, accepted at review= for an
  *unbracketed* span, reconstructed from loose guideposts.

*The gap is the third case, and it is the case that matters.* An
unbracketed span is unbracketed precisely because no session ever said
what it was working on — so it is the one span with no note to inherit,
and the label it gets instead says only *that* it was reconstructed. Yet
at review the human has just decided what it was; that is what accepting
it means. The information exists, in someone's head, at exactly the
moment the line is written, and is discarded.

*A second finding, and it argues against the naive fix.* The hand-edit
*destroyed the provenance*. That boilerplate is not filler — the function's
docstring makes a deliberate case for it: provenance is "the one thing a
reader cannot recover from the timestamps," namely that the interval was
reconstructed and confirmed rather than clocked as it happened, which
matters for a record whose trustworthiness is a stated project goal.
Overwriting it in place trades one irrecoverable fact for another. They
are not competing, and a typed annotation should *append* to the
provenance rather than replace it — =task definition, accepted at review=
keeps both.

*Open, and worth settling before building:* whether the prompt belongs at
mark time (while the span's evidence lines are still on screen, which is
the argument for it) or at apply; whether it fires for every span or only
unbracketed ones; and whether an empty answer is allowed to fall through
to today's behaviour, which it probably must, since a review pass that
cannot be completed silently is worse than a vague label.

*Not blocking anything, and deliberately small.* It changes what one
function returns and adds one prompt. Related to
[[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] by adjacency — that
heading put the evidence on screen, and this one would capture the
conclusion the human draws from it — but neither requires the other.
* Observability
:PROPERTIES:
:ARCHIVE:  DONE.org::* Observability
:END:

Broader than clocking — visibility into what every tool call
actually did, both after the fact and at the start of a session.

** TODO Make task lifecycle answerable from one timeline, starting at creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 10:32]
:END:

Prompted 2026-08-11 by a concrete reporting question — "how long did it
take from capture time to the PLANNING phase, for each of these tasks?"
— which is the first properly articulated demand on this project's
second stated goal, trustworthy tracking of where attention actually
went. It cannot be answered today, and the reason is not the one it
looks like.

*Measured 2026-08-11:*

: TODO.org  headings carrying :CREATED:   56
: TODO.org  "- State ..." lines            7
: DONE.org  "- State ..." lines            0

All seven come from the two hooks that write their note explicitly
(=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=, =--promote-planning-to-doing=). Not
one came from =org_set_todo=, which binds =org-inhibit-logging= to =t=
unconditionally for every state. So the transition history barely
exists, and in DONE.org — where post-hoc analysis would actually look —
it does not exist at all. That zero is *not* the archiver stripping
drawers: =org-archive-subtree= moves the whole subtree intact. Nothing
was ever written.

The example question is the worst case of this. =PLANNING= is transient
— auto-promoted to =DOING= the instant a plan is approved — and the
same census found *zero* headings sitting in it. Current state can
therefore never answer "when did this reach PLANNING"; only a
transition log can.

*So the blocker is the missing log, not =:CREATED:='s location,* and
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] already fixes it:
apply runs inside a genuinely interactive command, which is what lets
org's native logging fire instead of being suppressed. This heading is
the remaining half — making the timeline start at the right place — and
is close to worthless before the cutover, since there would be a
creation line and then silence.

*Proposal: duplicate =:CREATED:= into =:LOGBOOK:= as a creation entry.
Do not move it.* As a property it is cheaply queryable — org-ql filters
and sorts on properties directly — and drawer text is not. Moving it
trades an easy query for a parse. Duplicating buys one timeline and one
parser: analysis becomes "read the entries in order" rather than "read
a property, read a drawer, reconcile two timestamp conventions." It also
gives [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]]'s single
chronological drawer somewhere to begin.

*org already has the shape, so nothing needs inventing.* With no
previous state org renders the field as an empty string — =org.el= line
11092, =(or org-log-note-previous-state "")= — producing a native
creation entry with no previous state.

*Corrected 2026-08-11, twice, both by direct test rather than reading.*

1. *The literal above was wrong.* The empty string is padded, not
   quoted, so the real rendering carries no ="= pair at all:

   : - State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 15:46]

   A parser keyed on =from ""= matches nothing. The claim that "existing
   parsers, and org-ql, handle it unmodified" was never verified and
   should be re-checked against that actual string before anything is
   built on it.

2. *=TODO= alone carried no =!= marker*, so the one transition this
   feature most needs — into =TODO=, the default keyword a capture
   receives — logged nothing, silently and without error. Interactivity
   is necessary for native logging but not sufficient; logging also has
   to be *requested*. Fixed by
   [[id:90a5a0cf-a81d-45dc-a453-0bfe61e3ef93][90a5a0cf]]. Had this
   shipped as written, it would have produced no creation entries for
   precisely the common case and looked like a bug in apply.

*The alternative, which may well be better and is not yet weighed:*
synthesise the creation entry *at read time*, in whatever reporting
function consumes this, from the =:CREATED:= property that already
exists. Nothing in any file changes, there is no duplication to diverge,
and no migration. The cost is that every consumer must know to do it,
where writing it once puts it in the record for anything that reads the
drawer — including a human. Decide this before writing anything.

*If the write-it-down option wins, two consequences:*
- *Backfill.* 56 headings have =:CREATED:= and no creation entry. A
  one-time pass can synthesise them from the property — mechanical and
  safe, with precedent in the retrospective consolidation pass recorded
  in DONE.org. Without it the analysis has a cutoff date.
- *It depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]].*
  =org_capture= does not write =:CREATED:= at all, so headings born from
  the tool have no creation time for a backfill to read. Fix that first
  or those headings have no origin in either place.

*Mechanism, if written at creation time:* it need not be special. Under
the queue model creation can simply *be* the =capture= event proposed in
[[id:7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f][7fa68d5c]], with apply
writing its =:LOGBOOK:= entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
The two headings are one feature approached from different sides —
capture durability there, timeline origin here.

*One semantic caveat for whoever writes the parser:* "created" and
"acquired its first TODO keyword" are not the same event in general.
They coincide throughout this project because every task heading is born
with a keyword, but the equivalence is a local accident, not a rule, and
a heading that gains a keyword later would make them diverge.
** TODO Hook-injected context is only as reliable as the model choosing to relay it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c5b02503-1700-47fd-bf89-bd3296b24af2
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 10:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-14 Fri 10:32] \\
  captured from conversation, unexamined assumption
:END:

*Raised 2026-08-14, tracing where the session-start report is defined.*
Both =SessionStart= hooks work by injecting =additionalContext=, and
*nothing makes the model relay or act on it.* If a session reads the
context and says nothing, the outcome is indistinguishable from the hook
never having run — silent on both sides. CLAUDE.md already states the
expectation without noticing it is an assumption: the report "is injected
as =additionalContext=, which Claude is expected to relay to the user as
a question — the hook itself has no way to literally prompt."

*Evidence from the session that found it, cutting both ways.* The relay
worked: this session opened by surfacing
[[id:b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691][b5f94b88]] from the injected
context. But it worked partly because the user's first prompt was "what's
next?", which made the line directly relevant. Had the session opened
with a narrow request, the same line would have sat in context with no
reason to mention it, and the user would have had no way to know it
existed. n=1, and the one trial was the favourable case.

*Two hooks already depend on it,* and both are load-bearing:
=.claude/hooks/session-context.sh= (what was I last doing) and
=bin/hooks/session-start-recovery-check= (a CLOCK interval left open
before today, which needs the user to be *asked* when work actually
stopped — the whole point is a question).

*A third is about to be added on the strength of the first two.*
[[id:d7849119-8224-4993-a8ab-83e1434df53c][d7849119]] part 2 specifies a
=PostToolUse= hook injecting a reminder to check the sibling file, and
justifies the choice as mirroring "the existing =SessionStart=
stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
heavier-handed than this warrants." That reasoning is sound about
*blocking* and silent about *reliability*: it adopts inject-and-hope as a
house pattern citing a precedent whose relay rate nobody has ever
checked. Deciding this heading first is what stops the assumption being
copied a third time.

*Options, none chosen.*

- *A channel the human reads directly,* which removes the model from the
  loop entirely. The statusline is the only one that exists —
  [[id:4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5][4cda6bf7]] and
  [[id:290b6fc5-3ebb-4827-9a97-5a3542e354b6][290b6fc5]] both live there,
  and *neither names this as a reason to want them.* The fix exists in
  the codebase as a capability and nowhere as an argument.
- *Make the hook's own output user-visible,* if Claude Code surfaces hook
  stdout to the user for any hook event. *Unverified* — worth checking
  against the current hook documentation before it is either relied on or
  dismissed.
- *Accept it and measure.* Nothing today records whether injected context
  was relayed, so "usually works" is not a claim anyone can currently
  make. The audit log and the event queue both record tool calls; neither
  records what a session chose to say.

*The sharpest version of the problem:* the recovery hook exists to ask a
question only the human can answer, and its delivery depends on the party
that cannot be held to it. That is the same shape as the reason apply is
human-triggered — a mechanism whose correctness rests on the model doing
something it was merely asked to do.


** TODO Reconstruct coarse day-level attention intervals for the agenda :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 11:35]
:BLOCKER:  ids(df5afbbd-0eac-4032-be91-17cc22ef0cd7)
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-14 Fri 11:36] \\
  manual pass, no blockers found
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 11:46]--[2026-08-14 Fri 11:52] =>  0:06
- [2026-08-14 Fri 11:46]--[2026-08-14 Fri 11:52] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 12:07]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:36] =>  0:29
- [2026-08-14 Fri 12:07]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:36] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

*Named 2026-08-14, after establishing what the system does and does not
record.* The queue records *Claude sessions*; it has never recorded the
user's own attention. Clocking manually in Emacs writes a =CLOCK:= line
and nothing else, because the active annotation is this module's
apply-time addition rather than org's. So solo work lands in the same
agenda-invisible bucket as unattended subagent work, for an entirely
different reason:

| producer                    | CLOCK | annotation   | clocktable | agenda |
|-----------------------------+-------+--------------+------------+--------|
| Claude session, via apply   | yes   | active =<>=  | yes        | yes    |
| subagent, via apply         | yes   | inactive =[]=| yes        | no     |
| the user, clocking in Emacs | yes   | none         | yes        | *no*   |

Measured the same day: TODO.org holds 84 =CLOCK:= lines and 52 active
annotations, and the ~33 without one all predate the 2026-08-11 cutover.

*The intended granularity is coarse, and this is the load-bearing
constraint.* Stated by the user: "I expect my attention intervals to be
coarser and just a bulk representation of what did I do that day." *Not*
one annotation per CLOCK line, and not an attempt to align with the
recorded intervals at all. That rules out the obvious helper — a command
that annotates the CLOCK line at point — which was offered and declined
for exactly this reason. A day-level summary is a different artifact
from a reconstruction of the clock.

*Doing it through the review buffer does not work, and the reason is
structural.* Spans are reconstructed from =pause=/=resume= guideposts,
which come only from Claude Code's =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= hooks. No
session running means no guideposts, means no span to review. The buffer
can *correct* a span that exists (=e= for endpoints, =a= to assign) but
cannot *create* one for a period the queue never saw. Time spent alone in
Emacs is therefore outside it in every era, not merely before the
cutover.

*So this is manual work in Emacs, by design and by preference,* and what
would help is small: =RET= from the review buffer now unfolds the target
=:LOGBOOK:= (2026-08-14), and
=claude-code-ide-org--consolidate-drawer-at-point= will re-sort a drawer
after hand-editing — though it is not =(interactive)= yet, so it needs
=M-:= or a two-line wrapper. Org inserts CLOCK lines newest-first while
notes land in insertion order, so a hand-edited drawer drifts out of
chronological order quickly.

*Sequencing, worked out 2026-08-14.* Nothing pending changes how
intervals are *derived*, so nothing blocks this: =ab75d6d2=,
=d1cf852a=, =c954f650= and =2aeb65d6= are all irrelevant to it. The one
apparent dependency, [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]
(re-measure the guidepost gap threshold), is weaker than it looks: its
own data found span count flat across 1200-1800s with no observations
between 1061s and 2070s, so a re-measure is unlikely to change any
reconstruction.

The asymmetry that governs the order: the queue keeps every raw event
and can be re-clustered at will, while annotations written into
=:LOGBOOK:= *cannot be re-derived* — redoing them means hand-editing org
files. So do this after anything that changes derivation, and before
anything that puts raw material out of reach.

*One thing to avoid meanwhile:* =M-x claude-code-ide-org-archive-finished-queues=.
It is manual, so it will not fire on its own, but it has already moved
seven sessions (back to 2026-08-08) out of the review buffer's sight,
and its own docstring notes that a restored file which lost its
watermark supports "only all-or-nothing" reprocessing.

*The producer table above is superseded, same day it was written.*
=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation= used to branch on an
item's =:agent:=, writing an *active* timestamp for anything without one.
That is the row reading "Claude session, via apply | active =<>= | agenda
yes". The branch was removed on 2026-08-14 — measured: all 68 events of
one session carried =agent_id= nil, so every span it produced was
published to the agenda as though the user had been at the keyboard. Apply
now writes inactive timestamps unconditionally.

*Which dissolves the contrast this heading was named around.* The
paragraph above objects that solo work "lands in the same agenda-invisible
bucket as unattended subagent work" while Claude-session work reaches the
agenda. After the correction all three producers are agenda-invisible, so
solo work is no longer the anomaly — it is the rule. The heading's purpose
survives, but its motivating asymmetry does not; the reason to build
attention intervals is now that *nothing* populates the agenda, rather
than that the wrong thing does.

*Left behind, and this is the part that bites:* 55 active =<>= annotations
remain in TODO.org, dated 2026-08-10 through 2026-08-14, against 14
inactive ones written since. The fix stopped the bleeding and cleaned
nothing. Those 55 are agent-derived spans currently rendering in the
agenda as human attention — and they occupy precisely the namespace this
heading intends to fill. A first attention pass would be reading an agenda
that is almost entirely mislabelled agent activity, which makes the
cleanup a precondition rather than tidying. Tracked separately.


** TODO Record turns-per-interval as an attention signal, and decide where it surfaces :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:04]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:08] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:04]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:08] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07] \\
  surface undecided, analysis recorded
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] =>  1:00
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-15: turns per interval would reflect human
attention in a way clock duration cannot. Sixty minutes holding 2 turns
and sixty holding 40 are identical on the clock and entirely different in
attention — which is this project's second stated goal, not its first.

*It is not another set of data points, and that is the strongest argument
for it.* =resume= events already exist, one per =UserPromptSubmit=. The
metric is a *view* of data already recorded: no new hook, no new payload
field, no new failure mode, nothing to keep in sync. That is a materially
cheaper proposition than anything requiring collection.

*Count =resume=, not =pause=.* An interrupted turn emits no =pause= but
always had a =resume=, so the resume is the robust anchor.

*Measured 2026-08-15 against this session, because the foundation is worth
checking before building on it:* 22 =resume= events against ~25
string-content user messages in the transcript. Close but not equal, and
the discrepancy is systematic rather than random — harness-injected
user-role turns (=ToolSearch= "Tool loaded" replies, =/plan= output) and
mid-turn interjections. Also worth recording so nobody repeats it: the
transcript holds *276* =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results. A
naive count of user messages is off by an order of magnitude.

*Three candidate surfaces, in the order they were judged useful:*

1. *The =:LOGBOOK:= annotation line*
   (=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation=, config.el:3559).
   This is the answer for genuinely *post hoc* review: queue files are
   drained and archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks
   later. Not writing it there means re-deriving it from archives. Costs
   one token on a line that already exists.
2. *The review buffer's span-evidence block*
   (=claude-code-ide-org--span-evidence=, config.el:4525) — available but
   *partially redundant*. That block already reports absences ("nothing
   for 12m, 14:03-14:15"), and a gap list is the same signal as a
   distribution where turns-per-interval is its scalar summary. Judging a
   single span, the distribution is strictly more informative.
3. *A clocktable column via =org_clock_report=* — where aggregate
   post-hoc analysis actually happens, and the one place the scalar wins
   outright: turns/hour can be averaged across a week, and gap lists
   cannot be summed at all.

*Recommendation, not yet decided: annotation only.* Keep it out of the
review buffer initially, since gaps already serve the review-time
judgement better and that buffer is already carrying interval, label,
staleness flags, evidence rows, gap rows and overflow counts. Defer the
clocktable column until a real report wants it.

*Cons worth carrying into the decision rather than discovering later.*

- *The count is measured; the inference is the guess.* A turn may be
  "continue" or three paragraphs of design, and the difference is
  invisible. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the
  precedent for retiring a plausible-but-wrong number on exactly this
  reasoning, and that argument applies here with less force but not zero.
- *It inverts under autonomy.* A 40-minute agent turn counts 1, the same
  as "yes". Turns/hour therefore *drops* precisely when the agent is
  working hardest — correct as a measure of human attention, badly
  misleading if anyone reads it as productivity. Whatever surface carries
  the number has to carry that caveat too.
- *It reads 0 for background and subagent sessions*, which means "not
  applicable" rather than "no attention", and a bare 0 does not say which.

*Reframed by the user 2026-08-15, and it inverts one of the cons above.*
Attention is the *primary* measure for assigning attention intervals post
hoc — not a supplement to clock duration. Long agent-work intervals do
reflect agent productivity, but they are precisely the intervals in which
the human attends to something else: email, post hoc review, or another
Claude Code session in another project. Intense back-and-forth means the
human was at the keyboard interpreting responses, often alternating
between tasks and meta-tasks inside one session. Stated goal: better
attention-time allocation *at hourly resolution*. Imperfect by
construction, and that is accepted.

*So "it inverts under autonomy" is not a caveat, it is the signal.* The
con recorded above — that turns/hour drops exactly when the agent works
hardest — describes the metric working, not failing. A low-turn hour is
evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being
measured. The caveat that survives is narrower and worth keeping: the
number must never be labelled or read as productivity, because it is the
inverse of it.

*The larger consequence: attention is zero-sum across concurrent
sessions, so this is an allocation problem, not an annotation problem.*
If the human was driving session B while session A's agent ground away,
A's forty minutes should not be credited to A's heading at all — the
attention belongs to B. Annotating each span with its own turn count is
the easy half; apportioning an hour among the sessions that were live in
it is the half that answers the question.

*The data mostly exists, with one specific hole.* Queue files are
per-session and all land in =~/.claude/org-updates/=, which is
*user-global*, not per-project — so the directory is already the right
shape for cross-session comparison. Two things stand in the way:

- *Hooks are project-scoped.* Only sessions in this repo write queue
  files at all, so today the directory sees one project and cross-project
  allocation has nothing to compare. Moving hooks to
  =~/.claude/settings.json= fixes this, which makes
  [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] a *precondition*
  for this heading rather than merely a neighbour. Worth noticing that
  the portability work was motivated for an entirely different reason and
  turns out to be load-bearing here.
- *Queue events do not record which project they came from.*
  =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from, note, title,
  target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type and
  source — no =cwd=. The hook payload *does* carry =cwd= (verified in a
  captured =PermissionRequest=, 2026-08-14), so this is one line in the
  =jq= object rather than new instrumentation. Project is also derivable
  indirectly, since =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes
  it, but that is a fragile join where a recorded field is free.

*Hourly resolution is a real simplification, not a hedge.* It means no
per-minute precision is needed: bucket resume events by hour, per
session, and compare. An hour with 30 turns in session A and 2 in session
B allocates to A without needing to decide what happened minute by
minute.

*Corrected by the user 2026-08-15, on a misreading of "hourly
resolution" in the entry above.* Hourly buckets are not a bucketing
scheme. The *attention intervals are constructed manually*, by the human,
on review of the gathered data. "Hourly" is a rule of thumb for the
granularity of the answer to "what did I attend to today, roughly" — it
does not mean splitting anything on the hour for presentation. The
preceding paragraph proposing to "bucket resume events by hour, per
session, and compare" describes an automated allocation this project is
not trying to build.

*Explicit non-goal:* measuring how productive the human was using Claude
versus not using Claude. Nothing here should be shaped to support that
comparison.

*So the tool's job is to inform a judgment, not to compute an
allocation.* Constructing intervals depends on reflection about meatspace
events this tool cannot capture and never will. What it can capture
should be as informative and insightful as possible — *moderated by the
necessity of keeping those judgment calls a lightweight exercise*. That
moderation is a first-class requirement, not a nicety: a presentation
that is more accurate but heavier to read is a worse presentation, and
the tradeoff should be resolved in favour of lightness every time.

*This reverses the "annotation only, keep it out of the review buffer"
recommendation above.* That recommendation assumed the durable record was
the point. If the human constructs intervals *at review*, then review is
where the judgment happens and where the information has to be. The
annotation's job is narrower: record enough of the outcome and its
evidence that the call can be revisited without redoing it.

*Two design consequences worth fixing now, before anything is built.*

- *Never present a total that looks complete.* The tool sees Claude
  sessions and nothing else. Any figure implying a full accounting of the
  day is wrong in a way that invites trust — and the gaps are precisely
  where meatspace went, which is the part the human alone can fill.
- *Report the exceptions, not the measurements.* A dense stretch needs no
  explanation; the human remembers being at the keyboard. A long sparse
  stretch is the one that prompts "where was I?", which is the judgment
  call they have to make anyway. This is not a new principle —
  =claude-code-ide-org--span-evidence= already reports absences as gaps
  for exactly this reason, and its docstring says why: "an absence is the
  more useful half." Turn density should follow the precedent already set
  in this file rather than adding a parallel one.

*Which suggests the useful form is a recall cue, not a metric.* The value
of turn density is that it tells the human what a stretch *felt* like —
at the keyboard, or set running and left — quickly enough that they
recognise it and move on. A number they must weigh is heavier than a word
they can recognise, and the requirement above says prefer the lighter one.

*Vocabulary settled 2026-08-15, and the finding is that it was already
settled and this session drifted from it.* The user observed that "review"
names two different phases and asked for distinct signifiers. Searching
for a name turned up one already in the file:
[[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]], written 2026-08-14,
is phase 2 — and it quotes the user's own term, "I expect my *attention
intervals* to be coarser and just a bulk representation of what did I do
that day." That heading also records that doing it through the review
buffer does not work, so the two-phase separation had been found; only
the naming was missing.

Meanwhile "active intervals" appeared exactly twice in the whole repo,
both in text written *by this session earlier today*. That was a fresh
coinage competing with an existing term, and it has been corrected to
"attention intervals" in both places.

*The settled vocabulary, named by artifact rather than by ritual:*

| phase | produces            | lives in    | status    | the act            |
| 1     | clock intervals     | =:LOGBOOK:= | measured  | apply              |
| 2     | attention intervals | the agenda  | attested  | the attention pass |

The adjectives carry the distinction that matters: a *clock* interval is
measured by the agent; an *attention* interval is attested by the human.
That is exactly the difference between confirming what was observed and
saying what one was actually doing.

*Rejected: "logbook entry" / "agenda entry",* which was the user's own
first proposal and closer than anything this session had produced.
Rejected on three counts: =interval= is the established noun (334 uses
against zero for =entry=); "agenda entry" collides with org's own meaning,
where an agenda entry is a TODO appearing in the agenda view rather than a
span of time, and that collision would be silent; and "logbook entry" is
literally correct but too narrow, since the drawer also carries state
lines and annotations while the meaningful product is the interval. The
instinct to name by artifact was right — the artifacts simply already had
names.

*Explicitly out of scope, recorded so it is not mistaken for an
oversight:* predicting or constructing attention intervals automatically,
given more history, external signals, and practice. That may become
possible and is not being attempted now. The present goal is only to make
the human's manual construction better informed and lighter.

*Clarification added 2026-08-15 during a consistency review, because the
settled vocabulary collided with an org term and produced two ambiguous
sentences within hours.* "Active" is doing two unrelated jobs:

- *=active= vs =inactive= is a property of an org **timestamp**.*
  =<2026-08-15 Sat 10:00>= reaches the agenda; =[2026-08-15 Sat 10:00]=
  does not. This is org's own vocabulary and is not ours to redefine.
- *=clock= vs =attention= is a property of an **interval**.* A clock
  interval is measured by the agent; an attention interval is attested by
  the human.

The two axes are *orthogonal*, and the second is implemented using the
first: an attention interval is written with active timestamps precisely
so it reaches the agenda, and clock intervals use inactive ones so they
do not. That is the mechanism, not a synonym.

So "active interval" is ambiguous in both directions and should not be
written at all — it was the rejected coinage, and it also reads as "an
interval whose timestamps are active", which is a true statement about
attention intervals that says nothing useful. Two sentences in
[[id:df5afbbd-0eac-4032-be91-17cc22ef0cd7][df5afbbd]] used it in the
org sense and were rewritten to say "active timestamps" and
"active-timestamp annotation" instead.

Rule of thumb: say *timestamp* when discussing the mechanism, and
*interval* when discussing the thing being recorded. If a sentence needs
both words, it is probably conflating them.



** TODO Record the originating project in queue events :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       83b3cdd6-982a-4ae1-878c-79a8c941f638
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 14:32] \\
  cheap now, load-bearing later
:END:

=bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from, note, title,
target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type and
source. It does *not* record which project the event came from.

That is invisible today because hooks are project-scoped, so every queue
file in =~/.claude/org-updates/= is from this repo and the field would be
a constant. It stops being a constant the moment hooks move to
=~/.claude/settings.json= — see
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] — and at that point
the queue directory holds interleaved events from every project with no
way to tell them apart.

*The payload already carries it.* A captured =PermissionRequest= on
2026-08-14 held =cwd= alongside =session_id=, =transcript_path=,
=permission_mode= and =prompt_id=. So this is one line in the =jq= object
builder, not new instrumentation — the same shape as the =tool_use_id=
field already added there for the block events.

*Derivable, but do not rely on that.*
=~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes the project in its
path, so session-to-project can be recovered by looking at which projects
directory holds a given transcript. That is a fragile join — it depends
on a path convention this project does not own, and it breaks for any
session whose transcript has been cleaned up — where a recorded field is
free and permanent.

*Why it matters beyond bookkeeping:* attention allocation across
concurrent sessions
([[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]) needs to compare
turn density per project per hour. Without the originating project on
each event, the queue can say a session was busy but not which work that
attention belonged to.

*Open question worth deciding at implementation:* record raw =cwd=, or a
normalised project slug. Raw =cwd= is honest and free but varies with
worktrees — this repo already uses git worktrees for parallel work
(DONE.org's worktree entries), so two paths can mean one project. A slug
would need a normalisation rule, which is a decision rather than a field.
* Bigger swings
:PROPERTIES:
:ARCHIVE:  DONE.org::* Bigger swings
:END:

Significant directions this project has not committed to. Think hard
before building any of them. The first two are both about the
standalone/Warp-access subsystem from this session.

** MAYBE Decide what this module may change on a user's machine when installed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 11:54]
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, from a narrow question — how to set
=org-log-into-drawer= permanently — that turned out to have a general
answer: *a distributable package configures its own behavior, and does
not silently reconfigure the user's.* Worth deciding once, because the
module already crosses that line in more than one place and each
crossing is cheap to fix now and expensive to fix after other people
depend on it.

*The specific inventory, as of 2026-08-12:*

1. *Four global hooks, installed unconditionally at load*
   (=config.el:1869-1873=): one =org-blocker-hook= and three
   =org-trigger-hook= functions. Anyone who enables =:tools
   claude-code-ide-org= gets auto-clock-in on every transition to
   =DOING=/=PLANNING=, =NEXT= demotion, and sole-=TODO= promotion
   applied to *every org file they open* — not only this project's.
   These are opinionated GTD policies, and they are the bigger problem
   here, not the smaller one.
2. *A hardcoded assumption about one user's config*
   (=config.el:2702-2708=): the comment reads "With =org-log-into-drawer=
   nil (this user's setting)". The =let=-binding it justifies is
   correct and should stay — binding locally rather than globally is
   exactly the right instinct, and it is already documented as
   deliberate — but the *default* wants to be a =defcustom= rather than
   an assumption about whoever wrote it.

*The precedent already set, same day.* The =org-log-into-drawer=
question was resolved by adding =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= to this repo's
own =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= headers rather than by =setq=-ing the
variable. Verified: =#+STARTUP:= sets it *buffer-locally*, leaving the
global untouched, so the scope claimed matches the scope owned. That is
the shape the rest of this should follow.

*The module is not starting from zero here.* Eight =defcustom=s already
exist (=-queue-directory=, =-capture-file=, =-working-hours=,
=-query-files=, =-session-recovery-enabled=, =-audit-log-file=,
=-clock-status-file=, =-guidepost-gap-threshold=). Configuring its own
behavior is established practice; the gap is the global hooks and the
one hardcoded assumption, not the general habit.

*Genuinely open, and the reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=:*
whether a *Doom module* earns more latitude than a *MELPA package*. A
Doom module is opted into explicitly, per user, by editing =init.el= —
which is arguably consent to exactly these policies, and the hooks are
much of why someone would enable it at all. A MELPA package is a
library, and the same behavior on load would be indefensible. The two
targets may want different answers, or one guarded entry point
(=claude-code-ide-org-setup=) that installs nothing until called. Decide
that before doing the work, since it determines whether the hooks move
behind a toggle or stay as they are.

*Related:* [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] changes
one of these very hooks, so whichever lands second should not re-litigate
the other's decision.
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] owns the file-header
convention the =#+STARTUP:= line was added to.

** MAYBE Remote MCP access :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5adeb6c2-afc0-4472-9529-fff48f8ba825
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Off-machine access to the tools server and IDE-companion. This
session already got a =claude= CLI running directly in a Warp pane
on the same machine to reach Emacs (HTTP tools server +
IDE-companion lockfile, no vterm required). The obvious next step
is the same thing from *off-machine* — phone, another computer, a
CI job — but that means opening those servers beyond localhost,
which is real attack surface: arbitrary elisp execution against
your Emacs, today gated only by "it's on localhost." Don't build
this without designing auth first — bearer token at minimum, ideally
scoped per tool.

*Risk:* every registered tool call is unsandboxed elisp execution in
your live Emacs — a bearer token alone adds no TLS, per-tool
scoping, or rate limiting. Don't just flip the host to 0.0.0.0 and
call it done.
