the snippet, treat upstreaming as a separate future MAYBE.
** NEXT Make the org skill and its discipline portable to other repos :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 11:04]
:BLOCKER:  ids(befaed0a-18ed-445e-b997-8b9c2965ad45)
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 09:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 09:20] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-15 Sat 09:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 09:20] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 10:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 10:21] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 10:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 10:21] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 11:02]--[2026-08-15 Sat 11:10] =>  0:08
- [2026-08-15 Sat 11:02]--[2026-08-15 Sat 11:10] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 11:05] \\
  blocked on rules-loading verification
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 13:27]--[2026-08-15 Sat 13:35] =>  0:08
- [2026-08-15 Sat 13:27]--[2026-08-15 Sat 13:35] assigned at review
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-15 Sat 17:11] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-15: the org skill is meant for use on other
projects, and this repo's recursiveness — a tool for working on itself —
is a means, not an end. So: does the discipline observed here survive
outside this directory?

*Measured, not assumed: no, and more starkly than the question supposes.*
There is no =~/.claude/skills/=, no user-level MCP config, no user-level
hooks, and no =~/.claude/rules/=. =/org= cannot be *invoked* outside this
repo at all, because the skill lives in this project's
=.claude/skills/=.

*What actually produced the discipline, and what travels.* The skill is
the small part. Of six layers, exactly one is user-scoped today:

| layer                                        | lives in                  | travels |
|----------------------------------------------+---------------------------+---------|
| transition rules, "create a heading", journal | CLAUDE.md                 | no      |
| keyword/tag/=:BLOCKER:= conventions           | =.claude/rules/=          | no      |
| queue writing (pause/resume/blocks/todo)      | =.claude/settings.json=   | no      |
| =org_*= tools                                 | =.mcp.json= + Doom elisp  | elisp only |
| org skill                                     | =.claude/skills/=         | no      |
| reload transparency, footnote discipline      | =~/.claude/CLAUDE.md=     | *yes*   |

*The crux: the machinery is already global; only the activation is
local.* The elisp module is in the Doom config, the queue writes to
=~/.claude/org-updates/=, and tracked files come from
=org-agenda-files=. None of that is repo-bound. Hooks and MCP servers
*can* be user-scoped, so the queue could work anywhere without new
machinery — what is repo-bound is wiring and prose.

*Coupling in the skill itself is modest*, which is the good news: two
references to CLAUDE.md (which dangle in any other repo), seven to
TODO.org/DONE.org, three to =org_*= tools, one to the queue. Roughly 16
lines of a 504-line file; the rest is generic org syntax and editing
guidance that is already portable.

*The file-location constraint the user added, and it is the deep one.*
Other repos will have their own TODO.org/DONE.org, possibly at a
different relative path (=./.org/= was the example). This is *not* a
hardcoding problem — =claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files= is
=(or claude-code-ide-org-query-files (org-agenda-files))=, and the only
=TODO.org= strings in config.el are docstrings about the symlink truename
issue. It is a *discovery* problem, and it has three teeth:

- =org-agenda-files= is computed once at config load
  (=directory-files-recursively= over =org-directory=), so a newly added
  project needs an Emacs restart to be seen. It currently yields *2
  files* — that is the real scale of what this system tracks today.
- =org-id= resolves =:ID:= from that same universe. A heading in a file
  outside it cannot be found by any tool here, so every =:ID:=-scoped
  operation fails silently-ish rather than obviously.
- =claude-code-ide-org-query-files= is a single global defcustom, so it
  cannot express "this project's org files" per project.

*Three candidate mechanisms, unevaluated:* keep the current symlink
convention (each repo symlinks its org files into =~/org/<project>/= —
works today, manual, restart-bound, and already carries recorded truename
gotchas); per-project configuration via =.dir-locals.el= setting
=claude-code-ide-org-query-files=; or make =--tracked-files=
project-aware. The third is worth noting because *the hooks already
receive =cwd= in their payload* — verified in a captured
=PermissionRequest= on 2026-08-14 — so the queue could record which
project an event came from and apply could resolve IDs within it.

*The neat part, and the reason this is blocked rather than open:*
[[id:befaed0a-18ed-445e-b997-8b9c2965ad45][befaed0a]]'s path-scoped rule
turns out to be the *portability* mechanism, not merely a context-saving
one. A user-level =~/.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= with
=paths: ["**/*.org"]= costs nothing in repos with no org files and applies
automatically in ones that have them. That decision was made for an
unrelated reason and generalises exactly — but it rests on a mechanism
nobody has yet confirmed loads, which is why befaed0a is =WAIT= and why
this heading should not start before it clears.

*Two risks to design against rather than discover.* First, correct here
may be wrong there: "call =org_clock_in= when entering =DOING=" is sound
only where the queue exists, so portable rules must state their own
preconditions or degrade to "if the org tools are available." Second,
2026-08-14 demonstrated the failure mode live — when =emacs-tools= did
not connect, the session fell back to =emacsclient= and silently bypassed
the queue. The rule added against that is project-scoped, so in another
repo the tools would be *permanently* absent and the guardrail
permanently missing.

Closely related from the other end:
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]], which asks what
this module may change on a user's machine when installed — the same
user-scope question, approached as an installation concern rather than a
portability one. These two should probably be decided together.

*Mechanism proposed by the user 2026-08-15, and it exists — with one
correction.* The proposal was shared =.md= files referenced by both this
repo's CLAUDE.md and the skill, so that loading the skill elsewhere brings
its context with it. Two documented mechanisms do this, and =@path=
imports — the obvious candidate — do *not*: they load eagerly at launch,
and a path resolving outside the working directory additionally triggers
an external-import approval dialog.

- *Skills carry supporting files.* A skill directory may hold
  =references/*.md= that =SKILL.md= points at, read on demand with normal
  file tools. They travel with the skill. Neither skill here uses this
  today; both are single-file.
- *=.claude/rules/= supports symlinks, explicitly.* Per Claude Code's
  memory docs: "you can maintain a shared set of rules and link them into
  multiple projects. Symlinks are resolved and loaded normally." And
  "personal rules in =~/.claude/rules/= apply to every project on your
  machine," with project rules taking priority over user ones.

*So the shape is one canonical copy, two consumers.* Content lives once —
naturally in the skill directory, since that is the unit that travels.
=~/.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= becomes a symlink to it carrying
=paths: ["**/*.org"]=, which applies in every repo but only when an org
file is in play. This repo's CLAUDE.md keeps a one-line pointer instead of
a copy. Nothing is duplicated, and the skill and the rules read the same
bytes.

*Split the content by who must decide to load it,* because the two
mechanisms differ in exactly that: a path-scoped rule fires
automatically, while a skill reference is read only if the model chooses
to. Conventions (keywords, tags, =:ARCHIVE:=, =:BLOCKER:=) want the rule —
they must apply whether or not anyone thought to invoke the skill. Setup
instructions ("here is what a repo must wire up") want the skill
reference — they are consulted deliberately, once.

*Two things this does not solve, and the second is the larger half.*

First, a user-level rule applies to *every* project, including repos whose
=.org= files follow different conventions. "Do not add priority cookies"
is this project's policy, not a universal one. Project rules outrank user
rules, so an override is possible, but the default is now assertive
everywhere — the paths glob or the phrasing needs to carry that.

Second, and this is the limit of the whole approach: *shared =.md= files
reproduce the instructions, not the machinery.* A rule can say the queue
exists; if the other repo's =.claude/settings.json= wires no hooks,
nothing writes guideposts, no permission blocks are bracketed, and
=org_set_todo= is not registered to be called. The instructions would then
describe a system that is not running — which is precisely the failure
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]] took three sessions
to catch, in the one repo where the machinery *was* present. Making the
machinery travel is a separate move: hooks into =~/.claude/settings.json=
and the MCP server into user-level config, both of which can be
user-scoped. The =.md= plan and the machinery plan have to land together
or the first is worse than nothing.

*Merged in from =ae2d9b4a= 2026-08-15, which was the same task written
first.* That heading lived in =~/org/notes.org= — a tracked file, but not
one this session searched — and was created 2026-08-14 09:22, a day
before this one. It was found only when the assignment prompt offered its
title as a completion candidate. The duplication is recorded rather than
quietly erased because it is the second half of a lesson: a search that
covers =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= is not a search of the tracked set. Its
content follows; the heading itself has been removed from =notes.org=,
and everything about this belongs here, since it is about *this* set of
artifacts rather than about notes in general.

*Its framing, which is better than the one this heading opened with.*
Named on noticing that splitting
[[id:52ede10e-5e6f-4392-b31d-b9ce9b450c3c][52ede10e]] out of
=claude-code-ide-org= was the first time a heading had to leave the
project's perimeter and still be tracked the same way. The conventions
built here — =:ID:= on every heading, =:CREATED:= stamps, categories as
keywordless top-level headings, =:BLOCKER:= dependencies, the
state-transition and clocking rules, the org skill itself — were written
as *this project's* conventions. They are not specific to it. That is the
whole question, and it predates any of the measurement below.

*Four questions it raised, none answered yet, and only the first is
addressed by the work above:*

1. Does the org skill trigger correctly outside the repo that defines it?
   It lives in =claude-code-ide-org/.claude/skills/org/=, so a session
   started elsewhere may not see it at all — "which would make 'apply it
   in the wild' a packaging question before it is a conventions
   question." Confirmed 2026-08-15: there is no =~/.claude/skills/=, so
   the answer is no, and the packaging framing was right.
2. *Do the state-transition rules make sense without a clock?* Much of
   this module's machinery exists to attribute *work time*. Notes that
   are not tasks may want the =:ID:=/=:CREATED:= discipline and none of
   the clocking. Untouched by anything above.
3. *What is the archive target for a file with no project?* =#+ARCHIVE:=
   in =notes.org= names =DONE.org=, which does not exist in =~/org/=.
4. *Does one =notes.org= scale,* or does user-level work want the same
   category/epic structure =TODO.org= grew into?

Questions 2--4 are about user-level org work generally rather than about
this plugin, and may deserve to split back out once 1 is settled — but
they are recorded here rather than lost, since the file that held them is
no longer the place for them.

Related from the other side:
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]], on what this
module may change on a user's machine when installed.

*** TODO MCP HTTP server responds 200 instead of 202 Accepted to notifications :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]
:END:
=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--send-empty-response= (the
id-less/notification response path, e.g. for
=notifications/initialized=) replies with HTTP =200= +
=Content-Type: text/plain=, but the MCP Streamable HTTP spec expects
=202 Accepted= for that case. Confirmed live, found while bridging
Warp to this server: Warp's own MCP client fails with "Transport
channel closed" sending =notifications/initialized= against the
unpatched server; the Python =mcp= SDK's client (used by
=mcp-proxy=) instead rejects the same response with "Unexpected
content type: text/plain". Verified fix: changing just the status
code to =202= (confirmed transiently via =emacsclient= before being
persisted as a Doom config override) resolves both — a full
=tools/list= round-trip succeeded afterward. Worked around locally in
this project's Doom config pending an upstream report/PR.

:END:
No README.md exists in this repo today — CLAUDE.md is currently the
*only* documentation file, doing double duty as both agent
instructions and de facto human-facing intro (confirmed: this repo has
no other .md file at all). Decided against Claude Code's =@README.md=
import syntax inside CLAUDE.md (which would auto-load the full file
into every session's context, same as CLAUDE.md itself) in favor of a
small, deliberate amount of redundancy: a human-facing README.md
(intro/tutorial framing, quickstart, maybe a trimmed install summary)
kept separate from CLAUDE.md, accepting that the small overlap zone
(mainly the intro/description, per CLAUDE.md's own opening lines and
maybe an abbreviated version of its "Emacs integration" section) needs
manual syncing rather than paying the token cost of importing the
whole file every session.

Two parts:
1. Write README.md itself.
2. Add a =PostToolUse= hook (matching =Edit|Write=, filtered to fire
   only when =tool_input.file_path= is =CLAUDE.md= or =README.md=,
   read via =jq= from the hook's stdin JSON — same idiom as
   =bin/hooks/pretooluse-transition-guard=) that injects an
   =additionalContext= reminder to check the sibling file for anything
   that also needs updating. Mirrors the existing =SessionStart=
   stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
   relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
   heavier-handed than this warrants.

Revisit 2026-07-31.

*Note added 2026-08-05:* consider a "Known limitations" section in the
README, surfacing accepted-but-easy-to-miss gaps that today live only
as prose buried in CLAUDE.md — cited here by content, not line number,
since line numbers drift (confirmed: this note's original citations
were already off by ~10 lines within the same day). Two examples, both
deliberately left as low-cost/self-correcting rather than fixed, from
CLAUDE.md's "State transition rules" section:
- *"Known gap, accepted"*: the =ExitPlanMode= hook fires whether a plan
  was approved or rejected, with no reliable signal to tell the two
  apart, so a rejected plan can still trigger a stray =PLANNING= →
  =DOING= promotion.
- *"Cross-session guard"*: the same promotion only fires for the
  session that set =PLANNING= in the first place, tracked via
  =claude-code-ide-org--planning-owner-session-id=.

A human skimming the repo has no reason to find either today unless
they read that CLAUDE.md section end to end.

*Part 2 is contingent as of 2026-08-14, and part 1 is not.* The
=PostToolUse= consistency hook above justifies itself by mirroring the
=SessionStart= hooks' inject-and-let-Claude-relay pattern. That
precedent's reliability has never been checked — see
[[id:c5b02503-1700-47fd-bf89-bd3296b24af2][c5b02503]], raised the same
day — so building part 2 as specified would copy an unexamined
assumption into a third place. The argument recorded above is sound
about *hard-blocking being too heavy*; it says nothing about whether the
lighter mechanism works.

Writing =README.md= itself is untouched by any of that.

** MAYBE Evaluate choice of shell and standardize all project scripts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       84b7d8b3-5161-4146-9167-0e7e2100e365
:CREATED:  [2026-07-31 Fri 15:23]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-05 Wed 14:06] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/script-shell-standardization.md][Plan]]

Surfaced 2026-07-31 while relocating the statusline script
(=bin/statusline.fish=) into the repo: every other script in
=bin/=/=bin/hooks/=/=.claude/hooks/= is bash (mostly no extension,
one =.sh=), but the statusline script is fish — the only fish
dependency anywhere in this project, and one =future users= (per the
README/=CLAUDE.md= discussion) can't assume is installed the way bash
can be. Named =.fish= explicitly specifically so this deviation is
visible from the file listing rather than buried in the shebang, but
that's a stopgap, not a resolution.

*Update 2026-07-31:* option 1 (pure Emacs) below now has a real case
study, not just a hypothetical — see the sibling heading above ("Case
study: apply \"maximum Emacs\" to the statusline script", ID
e202e93d-cfa7-4d0a-8721-8f06428bc1e4). The statusline's =jq=-based
model-name extraction moved into Elisp
(=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-model-name=), and the shell wrapper
became =bin/statusline.sh= — plain POSIX =sh=, no =jq=, no fish — since
once the logic moves out, there's nothing left in the wrapper that
needs a fancier shell. The fish-vs-bash framing above is now
historical for this one script specifically (it's =sh= now, not fish),
but the broader project-wide decision (which of the five options to
standardize on) is still open.

Needs an actual decision, not just noticing the inconsistency:
1. Pure Emacs, given Emacs is already a hard dependency for every
   script in this project (they all end in an =emacsclient -e= call
   regardless of which shell wraps it). Rather than the shell side
   parsing stdin JSON (via =jq= or Python) and interpolating specific
   fields into an elisp expression string, write stdin to a temp file
   and pass *that path* to =emacsclient -e=, letting Elisp read and
   parse the raw JSON itself (native =json-parse-string=, no external
   dependency) and do all the field-extraction, decision logic, *and*
   output formatting — mirroring the temp-file convention this project
   already uses in the opposite direction for output
   (=claude-code-ide-org-write-*-report=). The shell wrapper would
   shrink to an near-unconditional stub: write stdin to a temp file,
   call emacsclient with its path, cat/format whatever comes back.
   Beyond just cutting the jq/Python dependency, this would eliminate
   an entire class of bug this project has already had to work around
   once (see =bin/hooks/session-pause=/=session-resume='s =jq -R -s .=
   JSON-string-encoding, specifically to avoid unsafe shell-to-elisp
   string interpolation) — if the shell layer never interpolates a
   parsed field into an elisp expression at all, that whole hazard
   disappears by construction rather than being carefully avoided each
   time.
2. Python instead of shell for some or all of these. Every hook
   script that parses the Claude Code JSON payload currently shells
   out to =jq=; Python's stdlib =json= module does that natively, no
   external dependency, arguably clearer than =jq='s own query
   language for anyone unfamiliar with it. Tradeoff: gives up the
   one-liner brevity these scripts have today for more verbose (if
   more structured) code, and Python, while extremely common on
   macOS/Linux dev machines, is still one more runtime assumption on
   top of whatever shell gets picked for the scripts that stay
   shell — this wouldn't replace the bash/fish/sh decision below so
   much as sit alongside it (e.g. "simple wrapper: shell; anything
   parsing/branching on JSON: Python").
3. Broader: is bash itself the right universal baseline for "future
   users," or should everything target POSIX =sh= for maximum
   portability instead?
4. Decide fish is fine for this one script specifically (e.g. if it's
   meaningfully clearer/shorter in fish) and document *why* it's the
   one exception, so a future contributor doesn't "fix" it back to
   bash without understanding the tradeoff, or
5. Standardize on bash (matching every other script) and rewrite
   =bin/statusline.fish= to drop the fish dependency entirely.

Whatever's decided should also cover: should this be written down as
a project convention (e.g. in CLAUDE.md's "Repository layout" or a new
"Scripting conventions" note) so it doesn't have to be
re-litigated per script.

** TODO Clearing a buffer's read-only flag for an MCP tool leaves it cleared :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 12:37]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] =>  0:23
- [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-10. None of the MCP tools bind
=inhibit-read-only=, so any of them fails outright with a
=buffer-read-only= error against a buffer the user has toggled
read-only (=C-x C-q=). The standing workaround -- documented in
CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" note -- is to clear the flag and
proceed without asking, since the user sets it to guard against their
own stray keystrokes, not to block Claude.

*The gap:* nothing ever restores it. Every clear silently disables that
guard from then on, and the user only finds out by noticing. The
workaround was written as a way past an error, and the other half was
never specified.

*Leading fix, not settled:* have the tool wrappers bind
=inhibit-read-only= t around their own writes, so the user's flag is
never touched at all. Strictly better than clear-then-restore, which
has a failure window (a tool erroring mid-call leaves the buffer
cleared) and races badly with concurrent sessions -- two of them
interleaving clear/restore can restore a flag the other still needs
cleared, or leave it cleared anyway. Binding has no window and no
shared state.

*The one thing it rested on is now verified.* Read-only guards
modification, not saving, so =save-buffer= ought to succeed under
=inhibit-read-only= -- but every tool here saves, and an unverified
"ought to" in the save path is how this project has acquired bugs
before. Confirmed in passing 2026-08-10: this very heading was inserted
into =TODO.org= while its buffer was read-only, using exactly the
proposed binding. The text reached disk, the buffer came back unmodified,
and =buffer-read-only= was still =t= afterwards. So the fix is a small
edit to each wrapper, not a research question.

Cheap and self-contained; no dependency on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]. Note the
refactor does *not* make it moot: after the cutover the review-and-apply
command becomes the only writer of live state, and it writes to exactly
the buffers the user is most likely to be viewing read-only at the time.

Two documents encode the current half-answer and must be updated
together with the fix: CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" paragraph under
the MCP tools table, and the =feedback-todo-org-read-only= memory.

*Raised in priority 2026-08-10 by the interactive apply hitting it.* The
first real =claude-code-ide-org-review= apply against a read-only
=TODO.org= failed outright; toggling =C-x C-q= and re-applying
succeeded. Two things make this a sharper case than the MCP-tool one
above, rather than another instance of it:

- *The gesture is the opposite of a stray keystroke.* Read-only exists to
  stop the buffer being edited by accident. =M-x
  claude-code-ide-org-review=, then =m=, then =x= is about as deliberate
  as an action gets, and it is the human's own hands doing it. The guard
  fires on precisely the case it was never meant to catch.
- *After the cutover this blocks the only writer there is.*
  [[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] makes apply the
  sole path by which anything reaches an org file. A user who habitually
  keeps =TODO.org= read-only while reading it would then find the entire
  system silently unable to record anything, with a raw
  =buffer-read-only= error as the only clue.

So the wrapper fix above should cover
=claude-code-ide-org--review-apply-item= too, not just the MCP tools.

*One property confirmed by the failure, worth keeping:*
=--review-apply= records only items that returned no error, so the
failed attempt marked nothing applied and the retry was clean -- no
double-application, no half-applied batch. Whatever the fix, do not
regress that.

Raised by the user 2026-08-12: nothing has been archived in quite some
time, and *certainly not since the queue cutover*. So the open question
about whether this file's hierarchy earns its keep — the user floated
flattening the source instead — should be judged on what a real archive
pass actually does, not on inference. Blocked on

epic's children are most of the current DONE backlog, and archiving it
mid-flight would scatter an epic still being worked.

*What is already known going in, verified 2026-08-12 rather than
assumed:*

- =org-archive-save-context-info= includes =olpath= and *is* writing it —
  18 of DONE.org's 22 archived headings carry =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:=. The
  outline path survives archiving. An earlier claim in
  [[id:cce72644-7d5e-4def-a165-0a3b815f482b][cce72644]] that archiving
  loses structure was wrong and has been corrected there; the section
  that vanished was removed by hand in =b716d37=, not by archiving.
- DONE.org is *not* in =claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files=, which
  returns only TODO.org and =~/org/notes.org=. So =org_query= and an
  unscoped =org_clock_report= do not see archived work at all — which is
  its own question once a large archive pass moves a lot of history out
  of view. Related: the still-MAYBE "Consider archives in
  org_clock_report's scope (agenda-with-archives)".
- But =org-id-find= *does* resolve into DONE.org — an archived
  heading's =:ID:= still resolves. Confirmed on =405e05b6=.

*The risk that asymmetry creates, and the reason this is worth a
deliberate pass rather than a bulk action.* The review buffer resolves
headings with =org-id-find= (=--review-current-state=). So a heading
archived while it still has *unapplied queued events* would later have
those events applied straight into DONE.org. That is arguably correct —
the record follows the heading — but it is untested, and post-cutover
the queue routinely holds unapplied events for exactly the headings most
likely to be archived, since a heading reaches DONE by way of a queued
transition. *Empty the review buffer first, then archive*, and confirm
the behavior deliberately instead of discovering it.

*That precondition originally read "drain the queue first," which is
unsatisfiable as literally stated* — corrected 2026-08-12 on measuring
it. A queue file never reaches all-events-applied: only events that
become *items* are ever marked, and =clock_in=/=clock_out= plus
unclustered =pause=/=resume= guideposts never do. Measured that day,
with the review buffer reporting "Nothing pending": =todo= 31 of 31
consumed — every state transition ever queued — against 14 =clock_in=,
17 =clock_out=, 109 =pause= and 113 =resume= residual, 253 events in
all. The achievable, and sufficient, precondition is *the review buffer
proposes nothing*, since an event that yields no item cannot be applied
into DONE.org either. See
[[id:78f485a8-e808-43ce-af5f-277a5c97c471][78f485a8]], which owns the
file-lifecycle half of this.

*Then make the judgment.* With a real pass to look at: does the
remaining hierarchy in TODO.org still earn its keep, or should the
source be flattened further? Note =b716d37= already flattened one level
by hand on 2026-08-04, collapsing =* Roadmap / ** Section / *** Task=
into =* Section / ** Task=, so this would be the second such pass rather
than the first — and the top level is load-bearing for CLAUDE.md's
"top-level headings are categories" convention.

*DONE.org's flat =* Done= is a choice, not a constraint — and the
alternative works.* The user, 2026-08-12: the
=#+ARCHIVE: DONE.org::* Done= line is what
produced that single top-level heading; they did not care for it when
they set it up but let it ride. *Hot-take preference: mirror TODO.org's
top-level heading instead*, so archived work lands under
=* Skill logic=, =* Tooling=, and so on.

*It can be expressed, and was verified end-to-end 2026-08-12* against a
scratch file, not inferred from the docstring alone.

*The wrong lever is =%s=.* In the headline half of an archive location
it substitutes the *file name*, not the outline path — =::* From %s=
yields "From TODO.org". No amount of =#+ARCHIVE:= alone can mirror a
source heading.

*The right lever is an inherited =:ARCHIVE:= property.* Per
=org-archive-location='s own docstring it is honoured "if found in the
file or in an entry, *and anywhere up the hierarchy*." With
=:ARCHIVE: DONE.org::* Skill logic= on the category heading:

| source                                     | landed under                |
|--------------------------------------------+-----------------------------|
| =* Skill logic= → task A, *and* a level-3 A2 | =* Skill logic=, created  |
| =* Tooling= → task B                       | =* Tooling=, created        |
| =* No property here= → task C              | =* Done=, via =#+ARCHIVE:=  |

Four observed properties, each of which matters to the decision:

1. *Inheritance reaches arbitrarily deep.* A level-3 heading routed
   correctly from a property on its level-1 grandparent — so one
   property per category covers every task beneath it, including
   [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]'s twenty-odd
   children.
2. *Target headings are created on demand*, so DONE.org needs no
   pre-building.
3. *=#+ARCHIVE:= still serves as the fallback* for anything not under a
   property-bearing category — adoption can be incremental rather than
   all-at-once.
4. *Depth is flattened within a category.* The level-3 entry landed at
   level 2, a sibling of its former parent. =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:= records
   the real parentage (=Skill logic/task A=), so it is recoverable but
   not structural. Top-level mirroring survives; sub-nesting does not.

*The cost, and it is the second instance today of the same conflict.*
This requires a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer on top-level headings, which
CLAUDE.md forbids. The same rule blocked =:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= earlier the
same day, which is why that fix went to =#+STARTUP:= instead.

*Argument for treating =:ARCHIVE:= as an exception rather than a
violation:* the rule's stated purpose is that categories are "pure
structure — grouping related tasks," and its concrete targets are
=:ID:= and =:CREATED:=, both *task* metadata whose presence would make a
category look like work. =:ARCHIVE:= is the opposite — structural
metadata about the grouping itself, saying where its contents go.
Narrowing the rule to "no *task* metadata" rather than "no properties"
would permit this without weakening what it was written to prevent.
That wording change belongs to
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] if adopted.

*One thing to decide in the same breath:* the existing 22 archived
headings all sit under =* Done=. Adopting mirroring leaves a split
archive unless DONE.org is reorganised retroactively — which is
mechanical rather than a judgement call, since every entry already
carries an =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:= naming its source category.

-----

[[file:~/.claude/plans/parsed-stargazing-lynx.md][Plan]] — approved
2026-08-17. Carries the sweep's mechanics and the ordering derivation; the
41-row completion-time manifest lands in this body when the sweep runs.


** TODO Read a heading's front matter, and take a partial :ID:, without a whole-body read :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2d9eeebd-4375-4790-8485-92b244236a21
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 17:27]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-14, from a concrete request the tools cannot
serve:* "show me the front matter of =f4e628ce= — sans body — and the
tree of anything below it." Both halves fail, for different reasons.

*What the surface actually offers.* =org_outline= returns *structure*
only: one line per heading with level, keyword, title, =:ID:=, tags and
a =[blocked]= marker. =org_query= returns *matches*: one line with
state, heading, tags, =:ID:= and file. Neither returns a properties
drawer, a =:BLOCKER:= value, a plan link, or any body.

*The partial-:ID: failure is worse than absence, because it misreports.*
=org_outline= with =scope= =f4e628ce= answered
=Error: no readable file at .../f4e628ce=: an unrecognised scope falls
through to being treated as a *filename*, so a truncated id reads as a
missing file rather than an unresolvable heading. Partial *titles* do
work — =org_query heading:"permission prompt"= resolved to the full id —
so title-to-id lookup exists and id-prefix lookup does not, which is the
opposite of how this project's prose, commits and conversation refer to
headings.

*Measured, because the cost is the argument:* =TODO.org= holds 111
headings across 10,251 lines — median heading 75 lines, mean 92, largest
405 ([[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]]). Front
matter is 5 or 6. So answering "what state is this in, what blocks it,
what is under it" by reading the heading costs roughly *fifteen times*
what the answer needs, and that read happened about twenty times in the
2026-08-14 session alone, twice as a 60-line window to see one =:ID:=
line.

*An honest deduction from that number, before it is used to justify
anything:* part of the waste was habit rather than absence. =org_query=
already answers "what state is X in" in one line and already resolves a
partial title; it was passed over for =awk= repeatedly that day. The
genuine gap is narrower than the measurement suggests — properties are
unreachable, and 8-character ids are unusable — and a tool built to the
full measurement would be built to the wrong size.

*Proposed shape: extend =org_outline=, do not add a third tool.*
CLAUDE.md's own design note argues the surface should stay small because
each tool's schema costs tokens on every request, so extending an
existing tool beats adding one:
1. =scope= accepts an =:ID:= *prefix*, resolving it when unambiguous and
   saying so plainly when not — and, either way, never reporting an
   unresolved scope as a missing file. No change to the output at all.
2. When =scope= resolves to a heading, that heading's front matter
   precedes the tree: =:CREATED:=, the =:BLOCKER:= *value*, the plan
   link. Today none of those three is reachable — the tree line carries
   keyword, title, id and tags, and a =[blocked]= marker that says a
   blocker exists without saying which ids.

*Root only, never per line, and not behind a flag.* Two corrections made
2026-08-14 while the proposal was being read back, both worth keeping
because the first reading was wrong in a way that would have damaged the
tool.

*Per-heading front matter would destroy what the tool is for.*
=org_outline='s stated value is being "roughly 40x smaller" than reading
the file; multiplying every line by a property drawer scales with heading
count — 37 lines scoped to
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]], 111 unscoped.
Scope-root only grows the reply by a *fixed* four lines however large the
subtree is.

*And it should not be optional.* A boolean is itself schema, paid on
every request whether or not anyone sets it — which is the same argument
that ruled out a third tool, applied one level down. Four lines is below
the size at which a knob earns its keep, and a caller who names a single
heading by id is nearly always asking *about that heading*.

*Deliberately out of scope: semantic matching.* The user asked whether
title matching is semantic; it is not, and it should not become so here.
A fuzzy resolver that returns the wrong heading confidently is the same
failure this project has now measured twice — see
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] on a guess that was
wrong more often than right, and the suggestion fallback removed the same
day. Prefix matching is decidable; semantic similarity is not.

*Sibling, not duplicate:*
[[id:915378ac-3bd4-461d-b0f0-83f67fbb3dac][915378ac]] is this problem for
the *human* at Emacs — an interactive jump-by-title command, which
already establishes that =org-id-goto= is exact-match by construction and
that this project's 8-character abbreviations are "readable and
completely unactionable". Same root convention, different consumer:
that one wants =completing-read= over titles, this one wants the MCP
tools to accept what the prose already writes.

*The complement of this request turned up 2026-08-15, and it is the same
missing surface.* Asked to "show me 4f85", the only route was
=grep -n ":ID: 4f8500e6"= to find the heading, =awk= to locate the next
=^\*\* = and so compute the subtree's end, then =Read= with the resulting
offset and line count. Three tools and hand-done line arithmetic to
retrieve one heading — and the arithmetic is the fragile part, since the
extent depends on finding the next sibling at the right level.

So both directions are absent from the same surface: this heading asks
for *front matter without the body*, and that request wanted *the body,
whole*. Neither is served, and they want the same scoping argument.

*The primitive already exists and is not exposed.*
=claude-code-ide-org--subtree-text-at-point= (config.el:1961) returns a
subtree from its heading through its last child as plain text, without
modifying the buffer. It is used on the clocktable path. Exposing it is
closer to plumbing than to design.

*And the scoping already exists too:* =org_outline='s =target= accepts an
=:ID:= to index only that subtree. Whatever resolves an id to a heading
there is what a body-returning tool needs, which is also what makes the
partial-=:ID:= failure above worth fixing once rather than twice.

*Worth deciding together rather than separately,* since one tool with a
mode argument may serve both: front matter only, body only, whole
subtree, or structure — the last being =org_outline= as it stands.


** TODO Jump to a heading by title, not by pasting a UUID :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       915378ac-3bd4-461d-b0f0-83f67fbb3dac
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 12:02]
:END:

=org-id-goto= wants the *entire* ID, exactly, and offers no help getting
it. Verified against the installed org 2026-08-11: it is
=(interactive "sID: ")=, a plain string prompt with no =completing-read=;
=org-id-find= resolves through the =org-id-locations= hash, which is
exact by construction; and the file-level fallback calls
=org-find-entry-with-id=, also exact. A miss is worse than a fast
failure — =org-id-find= re-runs =org-id-update-id-locations= and tries
again, so a partial ID costs a full rescan before returning nothing.

*Why this bites here specifically.* This project is unusually
ID-centric: every heading carries an =:ID:=, headings cross-reference
each other as =[[id:...]]=, and both conversation and commit messages
routinely name headings by their first eight characters — =720b2dcf=,
=f9f61c04=. Those abbreviations are readable and completely
unactionable. The gap is not org's fault; it is the cost of a convention
this project adopted deliberately.

*Proposal:* an interactive command that =completing-read=s over the
known headings, displaying *titles* rather than UUIDs, and jumps via
=org-id-goto= with the full ID behind the candidate.

*One design detail worth getting right:* annotate each candidate with
the ID's first eight characters. Doom's completion styles match against
the whole candidate string, so typing =720b2dcf= would then narrow
directly — which makes every abbreviation in these headings, in commit
messages and in conversation immediately typeable, rather than
something to look up first. That is most of the value; titles alone
would be a lesser tool.

*Building the candidates:* =org-id-locations= maps id to file and knows
nothing about titles, so they have to come from a scan. Either =org-ql=
over =claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files= — already a dependency, and
the same path =org_query= uses — or a plain =org-map-entries=. Do not
cache without measuring first: the tracked set is two files and well
under a hundred headings, so a rescan per invocation is very likely
cheaper than any invalidation bug it would buy.

*Not a gap, a convenience.* =C-c C-o= on any =[[id:...]]= link already
works and needs no new code, because the links in the file carry full
IDs. Scope this accordingly — it is a small command, not a subsystem.

*Testing:* the candidate-building function is ordinary and testable
(given a file set, assert the expected id/title pairs). The completion
UI itself is the fuzzy part and should be a documented manual check,
per this project's own "reasonably feasible" rule rather than forced
into a deterministic test.

** TODO Persist claude-code-ide-org-capture-file in the Doom config
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       bd482c92-a71a-4d7d-88df-47678ee94c79
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 21:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 21:21] \\
  persist the capture-file defcustom
:END:

Set live on 2026-08-11 via =M-:= to
=~/git/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org=, which is what makes
[[id:47c027d2-7b5a-4de0-baf4-f992f3b814bd][47c027d2]]'s reworked
=org_capture= actually aim at this project. It is *not* in
=~/.config/doom/config.el=, so it lasts exactly as long as this Emacs
process and then silently reverts to =org-default-notes-file=.

One line, in the =after! org= block alongside the other org settings:

: (setq claude-code-ide-org-capture-file "~/git/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org")

*Captured because nothing else would have surfaced it.* The only prior
mention was on =47c027d2=, which is now =DONE= — a note in a completed
heading that no future session reads. And the failure mode is quiet:
capture keeps working, it just writes somewhere else, exactly as it did
before anyone noticed. This is the inverse of the usual reload
precondition — the volatile layer is the one that looks permanent.

*Watch when it is made permanent:* this file is reachable by two paths —
=~/git/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org= and the symlink
=~/org/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org= that puts it in =org-agenda-files=.
Capture registers new ids against the capture path while agenda scanning
finds them under the symlink path. Both resolve to the same file so
lookups should work, but it is worth confirming that =org-id-locations=
does not end up with two entries per id, and worth deciding which path
is canonical rather than letting it depend on who wrote last.

*Measured 2026-08-15, and it makes the failure concrete rather than
abstract:* =org-default-notes-file= is =/Users/neil.best/org/notes.org=.
So the "silently reverts" above is not a vague risk — on the next Emacs
restart every *target-less* =org_capture= writes into =notes.org=,
the file that exists specifically for work belonging to no project.

*Narrow exposure, worth stating so the urgency is not overestimated:*
only target-less capture is affected. Every capture made on 2026-08-15
passed an explicit =target=, and those are unaffected by the defcustom
entirely.

*But pinning it in the Doom config is a stopgap, not the fix, and the
heading above should not be read as endorsing it.* A pinned value
hardcodes *one repo's path* into a machine-global Emacs configuration.
That is the same shape as everything
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] is about: the
moment a second project exists, the pin is wrong for it, and wrong in
exactly the quiet way described above — capture keeps working, it just
aims at the wrong file. Fixing the ephemerality by making the wrong
value permanent is not obviously an improvement on having it evaporate.

*The shape that would actually hold:* resolve the capture target from
context rather than from a global. The session's =cwd= is already in
every hook payload — see
[[id:83b3cdd6-982a-4ae1-878c-79a8c941f638][83b3cdd6]] — so "the org file
belonging to the project this session is running in" is derivable rather
than configured. That also removes the =org-default-notes-file= fallback
from the path entirely, which is the part that makes the failure silent.

*Recorded because the question was asked directly:* "why do you suggest
we pin the capture file in the doom config?" The honest answer is that
the suggestion was inherited from this heading and repeated without being
evaluated. It solves the ephemerality and creates a portability problem
in its place.


** TODO The org 9.7+ dependency is real, undeclared, and unenforced :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1ed7b2b4-a809-4547-b04f-099a3111fc1e
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 13:35]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-14,* on reading
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s probe results
and asking whether the org version it pins is a dependency, and whether
anything says so. Measured immediately, and the answer is that it is
real and nothing says so anywhere.

*The dependency, confirmed live rather than recalled.* Against the
bundled org that =emacs --batch -Q= loads on this machine:

: org-version: 9.6.15
:   org-element-type-p           fboundp=nil
:   org-element-contents-end     fboundp=nil
:   org-element-at-point         fboundp=t

Against the straight-managed org Doom and =bin/test= use: =9.8.7=, with
both bound. =config.el= calls =org-element-type-p= three times and
=org-element-contents-end= twice — all five in the drawer code — so on
9.6.15 the module does not degrade, it hits =void-function=. Both
accessors arrived in org 9.7, which is the actual floor.

*Where it is expressed today: nowhere that binds.* =packages.el= names
only =org-ql= and =claude-code-ide=, and never mentions org. There is no
=version<= check, no =org-assert-version=, no minimum in any header.
The only two places that know are indirect — =bin/test='s glob for the
straight =build-*= directory, and a troubleshooting paragraph in the
org-dev skill explaining why a test might behave as though a newer org
feature does not exist.

*Why that is worse than it sounds.* Both indirect mechanisms fail
*silently*. The org-dev skill already records that =bin/test='s glob
"silently falls back to the bundled org again with no error", and the
symptom is tests behaving as if a newer org feature is missing — which
is indistinguishable from the feature being broken. The glob does
resolve today (=build-29.4=), so nothing is wrong right now; the point
is that nothing would say so if it stopped.

*Not yet decided, and the options differ in kind:* an =org-assert-version=
or explicit =version<= guard at load time (fails loudly, on the right
machine, at the right moment); a declared minimum in =packages.el=
(documents intent, enforces nothing under straight); or making
=bin/test= refuse to run rather than fall back when its glob misses
(catches it in CI-shaped usage but never at a user's install). They are
not exclusive, and the first is the only one that protects someone
installing this module on an Emacs whose org nobody here controls —
which is the same argument
[[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]] made for keeping
the =command emacs= convention after the environment fix moved out.

*Related:* [[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]] asks
what this module may change on a user's machine when installed; this is
the narrower question of what it may *assume*. And
[[id:d7849119-8224-4993-a8ab-83e1434df53c][d7849119]]'s README would be
the natural place a stated minimum lands for a human reader.

** TODO Concurrent sessions share one git index, and explicit staging protects nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 16:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 16:22] \\
  user is blocked on this: no second session until a mechanism exists
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-17 Mon 12:11] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:95087d8f-e16d-45d3-bdea-9b179dc672b4][Lint for headings that are valid org but structurally unintended]] becoming NEXT.
:END:

*This one actually happened, deliberately, as a resilience test.* On
2026-08-12 two sessions ran against this same checkout. This session
wrote a heading to TODO.org, staged it with an explicit pathspec
(=git add -- TODO.org=), and went to commit. The commit failed with
=nothing to commit, working tree clean=: the other session had already
committed, and its pathspec-less =git commit= took the whole index,
sweeping this session's 69 staged lines into
=a8dc693 "Review-buffer interaction fixes ..."= — a commit whose
message describes unrelated work and names two unrelated heading
=:ID:=s. Content survived; attribution did not. =36410e2= is an empty
commit recording that, since =a8dc693= was already pushed and
rewriting a pushed commit under a live concurrent session is worse
than the mislabelling.

*The mechanism is the index, not the files.* Every convention this
project already has is about file contents — check for a modified
buffer, stage explicitly, never =git add -A=. None of them touch the
actual shared mutable state, which is =.git/index=: one per checkout,
not one per session. Worse, following the convention *increased* the
damage. Staging early loaded this session's work into the index
*before* the other session committed; a pathspec-less =git commit= then
picked it up automatically. Had this session not staged at all, its
work would have been left alone.

*Not covered by the existing worktree heading, and this is the load-
bearing part.*
[[id:2e09adb7-6e29-499c-a40e-98cd2b327cff][2e09adb7]] concludes that
worktrees handle the *code* side — "one checkout + branch per session,
no shared-file collisions" — and that only the org files need to stay a
single canonical copy in the main checkout. Read against this incident
those two halves collide: the file that got swept up *was* TODO.org,
which by that heading's own =:ID:=-uniqueness and clock-fragmentation
argument is exactly the file that *cannot* be partitioned into a
worktree. So a worktree session must still write and commit TODO.org
in the main checkout, through the main checkout's index, and hits this
identically. **Worktrees do not fix this scenario; they fix everything
around it.** That is new information for 2e09adb7, which assumed the
code side was the whole problem.

*Related but distinct:*
[[id:3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d][3cb3f955]] already asks
whether =git commit -- TODO.org= is "safe," but scoped to a *single*
session not sweeping up its own unrelated hunks in the same file. This
heading is the cross-session case: another session's *staged* work, in
any file. Same command, different failure. Worth answering together.

*One mitigation is already verified.* Tested 2026-08-12 in a scratch
repo: with =A.txt= staged, =git commit -- B.txt= commits B only, leaves
A staged, and does not commit A. So a pathspec-scoped commit is
genuinely safe for the *other* session's work. The catch is that it
constrains the wrong party — safety depends on the *committing* session
complying, and the session at risk has no way to enforce it.

*Caveat found while defining the hook below:* =git commit -- <paths>=
*fails on an untracked file* — =error: pathspec 'NEW.txt' did not match
any file(s) known to git=. A new file must be =git add=-ed first, and
only then can be committed by pathspec. So for brand-new content (a new
plan, a new script) the staging window this mitigation exists to avoid
is unavoidable, and exposure persists between the add and the commit.
The mitigation is complete only for already-tracked files — which is
where this incident happened, TODO.org being tracked.

*Options for something robust, none chosen:*
- *Convention only:* ban =git add= outright in favour of
  =git commit -- <paths>=, in CLAUDE.md and the org skill. Cheapest,
  verified to work, and binds only sessions that follow it. Given both
  sessions here are Claude Code reading the same CLAUDE.md, this is
  less hollow than it sounds — but it is guidance, not a mechanism, and
  the user asked for a mechanism.
- *A =pre-commit= hook* in =.githooks/=. Defined below — the only
  option here that is a mechanism rather than guidance, and cheaper
  than first assumed.
- *Route git writes through the event queue* of
  [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]. That queue
  already carries =session_id=/=agent_id= and already exists to stop
  concurrent sessions from mutating shared state directly, with a human
  applying batches later. Commits are the same shape of problem. The
  heaviest option and the most consistent with where the project is
  already going.

*The =pre-commit= option, defined.* Measured 2026-08-12 in a scratch
repo with a hook that printed its own environment. =GIT_INDEX_FILE=
identifies which command form invoked the commit:

| Command                  | =GIT_INDEX_FILE=            | Sweeps a staged file? |
|--------------------------+-----------------------------+-----------------------|
| =git commit=             | =.git/index=                | yes                   |
| =git commit --amend=     | =.git/index=                | yes                   |
| =git commit -a=          | =.git/index.lock=           | yes                   |
| =git commit -- <paths>=  | =.git/next-index-<pid>.lock= | no                   |

The hook can read *both* views at once: what the commit will contain
(=git diff --cached=) and what the real index holds
(=GIT_INDEX_FILE=$(git rev-parse --git-dir)/index git diff --cached=).
In the pathspec case those differ exactly as they should — commit
=B.txt=, real index still =A.txt=. =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= also
propagates into the hook, confirmed by printing it.

*Proposed rule:* refuse when the form is a whole-index one (basename of
=GIT_INDEX_FILE= is =index= or =index.lock=) *and* the real index is
non-empty; fire only when =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= is set. That is the
sweep case and nothing else. =git commit -- <paths>= always passes;
=git commit -a= against an empty index passes, since no one's work is at
risk; a human committing from a plain terminal is never affected. The
message should name the staged paths and print the pathspec form to use
instead.

*This retires the "needs ownership tracking" objection*, which the
first version of this heading asserted and which is wrong: the command
*form* is a sufficient discriminator and the safe form is
self-identifying. Per-session ownership of staged paths would only be
needed for a more permissive rule ("allow the sweep if everything
staged is mine"), not worth building first. If it ever is, the
precedent for where such state lives is
=claude-code-ide-org-queue-directory= — per-session runtime files,
deliberately outside the repo.

*Known limitations, all of them accepted rather than solved:*
- =--amend= is *indistinguishable* from a plain pathspec-less commit —
  both report =.git/index= — so it gets refused too, absent an escape
  hatch such as an =ALLOW_SWEEP= environment variable.
- =--no-verify= bypasses the hook entirely. This makes the mistake loud
  rather than silent; it does not make it impossible, and it still
  constrains the committing party rather than protecting the session at
  risk.
- Merge/rebase/cherry-pick paths are *untested* — the scratch-repo run
  errored before reaching them, and they need checking before shipping.
- Opt-in per clone via =core.hooksPath=, inheriting the same setup
  caveat =pre-push= already has.

*Testing* would follow the existing =bin/*-test= pattern
(=sync-plans-test=, =queue-append-test=): a scratch repo, each of the
four command forms, asserting exit status and message.

*Blocked on this in practice:* the user has stated they will not run a
second concurrent session until a mechanism is in place, so this gates
concurrent work generally rather than being one more backlog item.

** TODO Diagnose the 11 misplaced-heading reports org-lint found :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8d682a5b-1b03-4744-995c-a871a8b40ddc
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 14:40]
:END:

*Surfaced 2026-08-14 the moment =org-lint= was wired into
[[id:3bd3402b-3ab6-4ace-b20b-3ff84caa3fb2][3bd3402b]]'s script,* which
is the whole argument for having run it sooner.

=org-lint= reports "Possibly misplaced heading line" at =TODO.org= lines
3304, 3323, 4067, 4074, 4077, 4097, 4183, 6875, 7709 and 9785, and
=DONE.org= 743. None is diagnosed yet. (A twelfth report, a bullet-
counter complaint at =TODO.org= 822, is unrelated and also unexamined.)

*Why it is worth diagnosing rather than silencing.* One instance of this
exact shape was found by hand the same day: a heading glued to the end
of a prose line (=f39944f=, 2026-08-13), which org does not parse as a
heading at all — so it is invisible to =org-agenda=, =org_outline= and
every =org_*= tool, its =:ID:= is unreachable, and links to it dangle,
while the raw text still *looks* correct to a reader. If even one of
these eleven is that, a tracked heading is missing from the system
without anyone knowing.

*Expect false positives too.* The checker hedges in its own wording, and
these files quote org syntax in prose constantly — example blocks,
inline bold at line start, the transition tables. Sorting real from
quoted is the actual work, and the answer decides whether these become
errors or stay warnings.

** NEXT Lint for headings that are valid org but structurally unintended :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       95087d8f-e16d-45d3-bdea-9b179dc672b4
:CREATED:  [2026-08-17 Mon 10:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-17 Mon 09:54]--[2026-08-17 Mon 10:05] =>  0:11
- [2026-08-17 Mon 09:54]--[2026-08-17 Mon 10:05] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-17 Mon 10:05]--[2026-08-17 Mon 10:51] =>  0:46
- [2026-08-17 Mon 10:05]--[2026-08-17 Mon 10:51] assigned at review
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-17 Mon 10:11] \\
  would have caught a 6462-line break
:END:

Two checks =bin/lint-org= does not have, both identified 2026-08-17 after
a single line of prose re-parented 6462 lines of TODO.org and survived
two days, a full review-and-apply, and repeated clean lint runs.

*The incident, stated once so the checks have something concrete to be
tested against.* =* * *= was written into
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]'s closing summary
as a horizontal rule -- the markdown idiom, reached for without thinking.
In org a =*= at column 0 is a headline, so it became a *level-1 category
titled "* *"* which swallowed lines 3174--9636: sixteen level-2 headings
and the queue epic's thirty-four children, none of which belonged to it.

*=lint-org= reported =0 errors= throughout, correctly.* The file was
valid org the entire time. That is the whole point of these two checks:
the existing suite asks whether the file parses and whether this
project's conventions hold on the headings it finds, and neither question
notices a heading that should not exist at all.

*Check 1: a heading title that is punctuation only.* =* *= has no word
characters. No real heading in either file does, and none plausibly
would. Cheap, and it catches the exact shape that occurred -- at any
level, not just level 1.

*Check 2: a level-1 heading outside the file's known categories.*
Stronger and more general: level-1 headings in TODO.org are categories,
a small and slow-changing set, and one appearing mid-file is almost
certainly an accident. Would have caught this even if the phantom had
been named something word-like. Needs a decision about how the known set
is expressed -- enumerated in the check, or inferred as "every level-1
that existed at the last clean run", the latter being self-maintaining
but only as trustworthy as the run that seeded it.

*Where they go.* =claude-code-ide-org--lint-file= (config.el:5501) is
where the per-file conventions are checked; =bin/lint-org= is the 84-line
shell wrapper and needs no change. Per that script's own header, each
check wants a fixture that violates it -- "a lint whose assertions have
never been seen to fail proves nothing".

*Filed as one heading covering both* because they are one change to one
check suite, tested the same way, arising from one incident. Split them
if either turns out to want its own design decision -- check 2 plausibly
does, over how the known category set is defined.

*Related but distinct:*
[[id:8d682a5b-1b03-4744-995c-a871a8b40ddc][8d682a5b]] is about
diagnosing org-lint's *existing* misplaced-heading reports; this is about
adding checks org-lint has no opinion on.
